Relativistic magnetohydrodynamics in the early Universe
Alberto Roper Pol, Antonino S. Midiri

Abstract
We review the conservation laws of magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) in an expanding homogeneous and isotropic Universe that can be applied to the study of early Universe physics during the epoch of radiation domination. The conservation laws for a conducting perfect fluid with relativistic bulk velocities in an expanding background are presented (for the first time in their non-conservation form, i.e., as dynamical equations for the velocity and energy density fluid variables), extending previous results that apply in the limit of subrelativistic bulk motion. Furthermore, it is shown that the subrelativistic limit presents new corrections that have not been considered in previous work. We discuss the conformal invariance of the MHD equations for a radiation-dominated fluid and different types of scaling of the fluid variables that are relevant for other equations of state when the bulk velocity is subrelativistic. In particular, we review the super-comoving coordinates that have been proposed for matter-dominated fluids and present this choice of coordinates for any constant equation of state. First-order fluid dynamics to include imperfect relativistic fluids and the scaling of the transport coefficients with temperature in the early Universe are presented. We review the propagation of sound waves, Alfvén waves, and magnetosonic waves in the early Universe plasma. The Boris correction for relativistic Alfvén speeds is presented and adapted for early Universe applications. This review is an extension, including new results, of part of the lectures presented at the minicourse “Simulations of Early Universe Magnetohydrodynamics” lectured by A. Roper Pol and J. Schober at EPFL, as part of the six-week program “Generation, evolution, and observations of cosmological magnetic fields” at the Bernoulli Center in May 2024.



arXiv:
2501.05732

Introduction

In this work, the conservation laws of magnetohydrodynamics (MHD)
in the early Universe are reviewed,
described in general relativity over the
Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) background
metric tensor for a homogeneous and isotropic expanding Universe.
Since the focus of this work is the relativistic MHD description,
we only provide a brief introduction to
the FLRW model in Sec.
2
with the objective to make this work self-contained.
We refer the reader to textbook references on cosmology,
for example
Weinberg (1972)
;
Kolb and Turner (1990)
, or
Baumann (2022)
for
a recent textbook.
For extended work on perfect fluids and electromagnetism
in general relativity, the literature is extensive; we recommend
the reader the following textbook references
Misner et al. (1973)
;
Wald (1984)
;
Schutz (1985)
;
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Jackson (1998)
;
Carroll (2019)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.

The MHD equations in a cosmological expanding background
have been studied following the
pioneer work of
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Brandenburg et al. (1997)
;
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
;
Martel and Shapiro (1998)
.
In particular, the MHD equations during the radiation-dominated
era in the early Universe have been considered, for example, to study the
evolution of primordial magnetic fields
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Brandenburg et al. (1997)
;
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
;
Christensson et al. (2001)
;
Christensson et al. (2005)
;
Banerjee and Jedamzik (2003)
;
Banerjee and Jedamzik (2004)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2010)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2012)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2013)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2015)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2016)
;
Brandenburg and Kahniashvili (2017)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2019)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2017a)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2018)
;
Trivedi et al. (2018)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2020)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2020)
;
Perrone et al. (2021)
;
Dvornikov (2022)
;
Hosking and Schekochihin (2023)
;
Zhou et al. (2022)
;
Uchida et al. (2023)
;
Armua et al. (2023)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2023c)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2026)
;
Jedamzik et al. (2025a)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2024a)
;
Uchida et al. (2025)
, the production of gravitational
waves (GWs) from MHD turbulence
Roper Pol et al. (2020a)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2020b)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2021)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2021c)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2021a)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2021b)
;
Roper Pol (2021)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2022b)
;
Kahniashvili et al. (2022)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2022a)
;
Roper Pol (2022)
;
Sharma and Brandenburg (2022)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2026)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2024b)
;
Antoniadis and others (2024)
;
Caprini et al. (2024)
;
Caprini et al. (2025)
,
and the chiral magnetic effect
Brandenburg et al. (2017b)
;
Rogachevskii et al. (2017)
;
Schober et al. (2018)
;
Schober et al. (2020a)
;
Schober et al. (2020b)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2023b)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2023a)
;
Schober et al. (2024)
.
Excellent reviews on the generation, evolution, and observational signatures of primordial magnetic fields exist
in the literature (cf.
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
;
Subramanian (2016)
;
Vachaspati (2021)
and the textbook
Shukurov and Subramanian (2021)
).

The aim of the present work is to review the MHD equations in
an expanding background, extending previous work to relativistic
bulk motion and presenting new results on the fully relativistic description
of perfect fluids and resistive MHD.
In particular, we also show that previous work assuming the
\gamma^{2}\approx 1

limit in the
subrelativistic regime (cf. the original work
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
, or the
reviews
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
;
Subramanian (2016)
;
Vachaspati (2021)

and references therein)
ignores time derivatives of the Lorentz factor,
\gamma
,
which can lead to additional non-linear terms of order

U^{2}/\ell
, being
U
and
\ell
a characteristic velocity and length scale of the fluid, respectively, when the fluid is dominated by radiation.
Note that this term is of the same order
as the convective derivative,
(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\sim U^{2}/\ell
, which is of pivotal
importance to describe, for example, non-linear fluid dynamics and turbulence.
Therefore,
the time derivative of
\gamma^{2}
is not, in general,
negligible in a non-linear
description of subrelativistic fluid dynamics or MHD.

Many of the results in this work were presented by one of the
authors in the lectures of the EPFL course ‘‘Simulations
of Early Universe Magnetohydrodynamics,’’ in particular the theory lectures
on ‘‘MHD in an expanding Universe,’’
as a part of the six-week program ‘‘Generation, evolution, and
observations of cosmological magnetic fields’’ at
the Bernoulli Center for Fundamental Studies.
1
1

1

“Generation, evolution, and
observations of cosmological magnetic fields”
program at the Bernoulli Center at EPFL (Apr. 28–Jun. 7, 2024).

With the growing interest in studying the evolution of primordial magnetic
fields, fluid perturbations, and GWs, among others, in the
early Universe, the results presented in this work can be used for
a broad range of cosmological applications.
Many of these studies have been performed in the subrelativistic limit
using the open-source

Pencil Code

Brandenburg and others (2021)
, adapted to a radiation-dominated fluid with
a constant equation of state
p=c_{\rm s}^{2}\rho
, where
p
is the pressure,
\rho
is
the total energy density, and
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
is the square of the speed of sound, following the
pioneer work of
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
.
Current developments for including relativistic MHD in an
expanding background in
Pencil Code

are in progress by the authors and collaborators
Brandenburg and Roper Pol ()
.
In addition, the open-source
{\cal C}
osmo
{\cal L}
attice
Figueroa et al. (2021)
;
Figueroa et al. (2023)
is also being developed to
include an MHD description for early Universe studies
Figueroa et al. (2024)
;
Figueroa et al. ()
.
In this work, we present the theoretical framework for
MHD studies in the early Universe
going beyond the currently studied subrelativistic
limit of bulk fluid motion, and present
new results that also lead to
corrections in the subrelativistic limit that should be addressed
in future studies.

In Sec.
2
, the FLRW geometry of an isotropic
and homogeneous expanding Universe is reviewed.
This is considered to describe
the metric tensor over which the MHD conservation laws are studied.
The perturbations of the metric tensor induced by the velocity and magnetic
fields are assumed to be subdominant, such that the backreaction
of the metric tensor perturbations on the MHD equations can be neglected.
In Sec.
3
, the conservation laws of a fully relativistic
perfect fluid in the absence of electromagnetic fields
on an expanding Universe are presented.
Linear perturbations of these equations in the form of sound waves
are described in Sec.
3.7
.
The extension to imperfect fluids (with viscous and heat
transfer contributions) is then discussed in Sec.
4
,
and a review of the estimate of the shear and bulk viscosities,
and of the thermal conductivity of the primordial plasma is presented
in Sec.
4.2
.
In Sec.
5
, Maxwell equations in an expanding background
are reviewed, introducing the definitions of
comoving electromagnetic fields and current density (Sec.
5.2
),
together with the covariant formulation of the generalized Ohm’s law (Sec.
5.3
),
required to describe the current density for relativistic fluid
bulk motion.
We review the values of the conductivity in the primordial
Universe and show that its ratio to the Hubble time is in general
very large, justifying the usual MHD description where the
displacement current is neglected.
In Sec.
6
, the MHD conservation laws in an expanding Universe for a relativistic charged
fluid are described, combining the equations of motion of the fluid with
Maxwell equations.
Linear MHD perturbations in an expanding Universe are studied
in Secs.
6.5
–
6.7
.
In Sec.
6.6
, the Boris correction is introduced, which allows to correct
the Alfvén speed when it becomes relativistic, even in the subrelativistic
limit of bulk flow velocities.
We conclude and summarize the set of equations presented in this work in Sec.
7
.

In the present work, a few assumptions are made to simplify the MHD
description that can be relevant for some cosmological applications:

i)

Contributions from neutrino and electron free-streaming below
the Silk damping scales are ignored.
Their impact on MHD has been studied in the literature
(cf.
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Brandenburg et al. (1997)
;
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
for pioneer work).
The Silk damping scale becomes larger towards the onset of
matter domination in the early Universe and, hence, it needs to be taken
into account to study the correct evolution of large-scale
primordial magnetic fields across the early Universe, especially towards
the epoch of recombination.

ii)

The study of imperfect fluids
in this work is only presented using a covariant
description of Navier-Stokes viscosity
and Fourier’s thermal conductivity,
following the classical irreversible thermodynamics (CIT)
approach

Weinberg (1971)
;
Weinberg (1972)
;
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
;
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.
It is known that this description leads to acausal fluid perturbations
and a relativistic description of viscous fluid dynamics is an active
field of research (cf. the review
Romatschke (2010)
and references therein).

iii)

At high energies, asymmetries between left and right-handed particles
can lead to a chiral induced current
Joyce and Shaposhnikov (1997)
that can
produce vorticity and drive magnetic
fields in processes known as the chiral vortical and magnetic effects.
The latter effect has been studied in the context of primordial magnetic fields
using MHD simulations
(cf.
Brandenburg et al. (2017b)
;
Rogachevskii et al. (2017)
;
Schober et al. (2018)
for pioneer work).

Conventions and notation.

Natural units with
c=\hbar=1
and
Heaviside-Lorentz units with
\mu_{0}=1
for electromagnetic fields are considered along
the text.
In these units, the Maxwell equations in Minkowski space-time are

\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle J$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle J$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle J$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle J$}}{}\,,\qquad\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{}=J^{0}\,,\qquad\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{}\,,\qquad\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{}=0\,.

(1)

In general,
t
and
\tau
are used for cosmic and conformal time, respectively.
Derivatives with respect to
t
and
\tau
are denoted by a dot (e.g.,
\dot{a}
) and a prime (e.g.,
a^{\prime}
), respectively.
The early Universe geometry is described by the
homogeneous, isotropic, and spatially flat Friedmann-Lemaître-Robertson-Walker (FLRW) metric tensor,
g_{\mu\nu}=a^{2}\eta_{\mu\nu}

with
\eta_{\mu\nu}=m\,{\rm diag}\{-1,1,1,1\}
being the Minkowski metric tensor.
We will always assume
that the MHD perturbations are small compared to the background,
such that their effect on the metric does not feedback into
the MHD equations.
The signature is
m\,(-\,+\,+\,+)
, such that
m=\pm 1
allows
to keep track of the signature-dependent quantities.
Taking
m=1
, space-time intervals are spacelike and it is more commonly used
in general relativity, for example in
Misner et al. (1973)
;
Wald (1984)
;
Carroll (2019)
,
while
m=-1
yields timelike intervals and it is more common in
particle physics and cosmology, for example in
Baumann (2022)
;
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
.
In Sec.
2
, a generic signature will be considered,
while in the remaining of the
text, when the equations of motion are computed,
m=1
will be used for
compactness of the calculations as the
resulting equations of motion are not affected by the signature choice.

In most of the text, the coordinates are

X^{0}=\tau
and
X^{i}=x^{i}
with
x^{i}
being the comoving space coordinates.
Only in Sec.
2
,
X^{0}
is allowed
to be a generic
\alpha
-time, such that

a^{\alpha}{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}={\rm\,d}t
, which reduces to cosmic or conformal
when
\alpha=0
or 1, respectively.
When we describe spatial vectors in flat space-time,
we indistinguishably use upper or lower indices as the indices can be risen or lowered
with Minkowski metric tensor
\eta^{ij}=\delta^{ij}
in the
m=1
signature,
e.g.,
u^{i}=u_{i}
or
\partial^{i}=\partial_{i}
.
We will still keep both upper and lower indices because
Einstein summation is only assumed over one lower and one upper index, e.g.,
u^{2}=u^{i}u_{i}
.

1.1
Relativistic MHD equations in an expanding Universe

For the impatient reader, let us present the set of relativistic MHD equations (conservation of energy and momentum) in an expanding Universe describing the evolution of the total energy density

\rho
and the peculiar velocity field
\textstyle u

that we find in this work
(see Sec.
6
).
Up to our knowledge, the fluid equations of motion
have not been fully considered in this form

2

The set of relativistic MHD equations
are usually considered in their conservation form (cf.
Font (2008)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
), describing the evolution of the stress-energy
tensor components, or using different combinations of the fluid variables.
In this work, we also present these equations in terms of the primitive fluid variables
\rho
and
\textstyle u

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1-3c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\,(1+u^{2})\,{\cal H}\,,

\displaystyle D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})\gamma^{2}}\biggl[c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+c_{\rm s}^{2}\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}-\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggl(\tilde{f}_{\rm tot}^{0}-\frac{2c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}\biggr)+(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,{\cal H}\biggr]

(2b)

where
D_{\tau}=\partial_{\tau}+u_{i}\partial^{i}
is the material derivative,

\tau
corresponds to conformal time,
a
is the scale factor, and
{\cal H}=a^{\prime}/a
is the conformal Hubble rate.
The velocity field
\textstyle u

corresponds to the peculiar velocity with
respect to the Hubble observer
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}={\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}(\tau)/{\rm\,d}\tau
, i.e., comoving with the Universe expansion (see Sec.
2.4
).
The comoving energy density and pressure are
\tilde{\rho}=a^{4}\rho
and
\tilde{p}=a^{4}p
, respectively, where a constant
equation of state is considered to describe their ratio,
\tilde{p}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{\rho}
.
Comoving variables and spatial coordinates
\textstyle x
,
together with conformal time, are chosen to exploit
the conformal invariance of the equations for a radiation-dominated fluid with

c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}

Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Subramanian (1997)
(see Sec.
3
and, in particular,
Sec.
3.3
),
as can be seen by the fact that no
dependence on
a
is left in Eqs. (
2
)
when
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.

We note that when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)
,
an additional term proportional
to
\textstyle u
appears in the momentum equation, which is not present in
usual MHD (i.e., in fluids predominantly composed by massive particles,
with
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
), as well as modifications in some coefficients
of the different terms that depend on
c_{\rm s}^{2}
.
The appearance of this additional term is a clear indication that,
even when the equations become conformally flat for
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
,
the MHD dynamics in the early Universe could be modified with respect
to usual MHD
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
.
An important aspect of these modifications is that they
can, for example, lead to the production of
vorticity,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}
, from an initial curl-free configuration of the velocity field,
even in the absence of
external forcing (i.e., for the modified Euler equations).
Therefore, vorticity, defined as
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}
, is no longer a topological invariant, as for the
usual Euler equations when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1

Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.
This has been studied in the subrelativistic limit in
Dahl et al. (2022)
;
Dahl et al. (2024)
;
Caprini et al. ()
and in the relativistic limit in
Caprini et al. ()
.
The equation describing the conservation of vorticity can be found by taking
the curl of Eq. (
2b
) (see details in App.
A
),

\displaystyle D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}\Psi}{(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})\gamma^{2}}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\Biggl[\frac{\Psi}{(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})\gamma^{2}}\Biggr]

\displaystyle\,+\frac{(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot})\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2})}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}^{2}\gamma^{4}}+\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}\,,

(3)

where
\Psi
is given in Eq. (
299
),

\Psi=c_{\rm s}^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+c_{\rm s}^{2}\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}-\frac{\tilde{f}_{\rm tot}^{0}}{\tilde{\rho}}+\frac{2\,c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}}{\tilde{\rho}}+(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1){\cal H}\,.

(4)

Indeed, from Eq. (
3
) one can see that the second term of the right-hand side can lead
to the production of vorticity when the initial vorticity is zero in the absence of
external forces and baroclinic terms, i.e., when
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}=0
and

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2})=0

Caprini et al. ()
.

The comoving four-force
\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{\rm tot}=a^{6}f^{\mu}_{\rm tot}
can correspond to any
force applied to the fluid and it comes from additional
contributions to the stress-energy tensor on top of those corresponding
to a perfect fluid
T^{\mu\nu}_{\rm pf}
.
In particular, we study in Sec.
4
the inclusion of out-of-local-thermal-equilibrium effects like viscosity and heat transfer
by incorporating a deviatoric tensor
\Pi^{\mu\nu}
, such that

T^{\mu\nu}=T^{\mu\nu}_{\rm pf}-\Pi^{\mu\nu}
, yielding
an imperfect (viscous) force
f^{\nu}_{{\rm ipf}}=\partial_{\mu}\Pi^{\mu\nu}
.
The inclusion of electromagnetic forces to the fluid is required for the
study of MHD (see Sec.
6
).
It can be described by incorporating the electromagnetic stress-energy tensor,
T^{\mu\nu}\to T^{\mu\nu}+T^{\mu\nu}_{\rm EM}
, which leads to the
inclusion of
the Lorentz force
f^{\nu}_{\rm Lor}=-\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}_{\rm EM}
(see Sec.
6.2
).
When electromagnetic fields are included, Eqs. (
2
)
become coupled to Maxwell equations in an expanding background (see Sec.
5
),
which can be expressed for the Hubble observer in
the following way (using the temporal or Weyl gauge
A_{0}=0
and defining the magnetic
vector potential
\textstyle A
, such that
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}
),

\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{}\,,\qquad\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}=\tilde{J}^{0}\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}\,,

(5)

where
\textstyle\tilde{E}
and
\textstyle\tilde{B}
are the comoving electric and magnetic fields, related to the covariant components of the
Faraday tensor,
\tilde{E}_{i}=F_{i0}
and
\tilde{B}^{i}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\varepsilon^{ijk}F_{jk}
with

F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}\,.

(6)

The comoving four-current
\tilde{J}^{\mu}=a^{4}J^{\mu}

is described by
the generalized Ohm’s law [see Sec.
5.3
and, in particular, Eq. (
188
)].

1.2
MHD equations in the limit of subrelativistic bulk motion

In the limit of subrelativistic bulk motion,
u^{2}\ll 1
,
Eqs. (
2
) reduce to

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,=

\displaystyle\,-(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-(1-c_{\rm s}^{2})(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}(\tilde{f}^{0}_{\rm tot}-2\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot})+(1-3c_{\rm s}^{2})\,{\cal H}\,,

(7a)

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\biggl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\biggr]-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggl(\tilde{f}^{0}_{\rm tot}-\frac{2c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}\biggr)

\displaystyle\,-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}}{\tilde{\rho}}+(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}\,,

(7b)

and Eq. (
3
) reduces to

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\omega$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\omega$}}{}c_{\rm s}^{2}\Psi+c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\Psi+\frac{(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot})\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}}\,.

(8)

A Hubble friction term proportional to
(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,{\cal H}
appears
when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\neq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}

in both the momentum and energy conservation equations as a consequence of the Universe
expansion, breaking the conformal invariance of the equations.
Note that in the subrelativistic limit,
the Hubble-dependent term in the energy equation
vanishes if one rescales
\tilde{\rho}=a^{3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})}\rho
, as it is
shown in Sec.
3.3
.
The terms
\tilde{f}^{0}_{\rm tot}
and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm tot}
correspond to
energy dissipation and power exerted by the different forces on the fluid.
In addition, we find that
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}
, omitted in previous
work,
presents terms that are non-negligible even in the subrelativistic
limit (see Sec.
3
and, in particular, Sec.
3.5
), yielding a modification
in the coefficient of
(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}
in the
energy equation
3
3

3

We note that the corresponding correction to Eq. (
7a
)
was recently
pointed out in
Dahl et al. (2024)
in Minkowski space-time
before this work was published.
[cf. Eq. (
7a
)] and in the coefficient of
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}
in the momentum equation [cf. Eq. (
7b
)].

2
Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker background

At large distances in our Universe, the distribution of galaxies becomes homogeneous and
isotropic as we look farther away from us.
This is known as the cosmological principle and its strongest observation evidence
is the uniformity of the cosmic microwave background (CMB), with observed
anisotropies in the CMB temperature being only of a
few parts in
10^{5}

Durrer (2008)
.
Under the assumptions of homogeneity and isotropy, Einstein field
equations have an exact solution, corresponding to the
Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) metric tensor.

In this section, we give a brief review of the FLRW model that will be
used in the rest of this work to describe the background metric over which
the equations of motion for fluids and electromagnetic fields are
described in an expanding Universe.
For further details on cosmology and general relativity,
the reader is referred to the extensive textbook literature in
these fields, for example
Weinberg (1972)
;
Kolb and Turner (1990)
;
Baumann (2022)
;
Misner et al. (1973)
;
Wald (1984)
;
Schutz (1985)
;
Jackson (1998)
;
Carroll (2019)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.

2.1
Geometry with cosmic time as
X^{0}

We can start choosing our space-time coordinates as
X^{\mu}=(t,x^{i})
being

t
the cosmic time and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}=x^{i}
the comoving spatial coordinates.
The line element is described by the metric tensor
g^{\mu\nu}
,

{\rm\,d}s^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}{\rm\,d}X^{\nu}\,,

(9)

where
g_{\mu\nu}
corresponds to the Minkowski metric tensor

\eta_{\mu\nu}=m\,{\rm diag}\{-1,1,1,1\}
in special
relativity.
In general relativity, the metric tensor is a dynamical variable,
coupled to the distribution of matter and energy via
the Einstein equations that relate the space-time
geometry and the stress-energy distribution.
However, exploiting the homogeneous and isotropic geometry of the Universe at large scales,
the dependence of
g_{\mu\nu}
is reduced to the time dependence of a scale factor
a
.
Space-time can then be foliated in spatial hypersurfaces that can have positive, negative,
or zero curvature, depending on whether the space-line element can be given as a 3-sphere
embedded in 4-dimensional Euclidean space, as a hyperboloid embedded in 4-dimensional Lorentzian space,
or directly as the 3-dimensional Euclidean space.
Unifying all cases, the spatial contribution to the
line element is

{\rm\,d}{\ell}^{2}=g_{ij}{\rm\,d}x^{i}{\rm\,d}x^{j}=a^{2}\,\gamma_{ij}{\rm\,d}x^{i}{\rm\,d}x^{j}\,,\qquad{\rm with\ }\gamma_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+k\frac{x_{i}x_{j}}{1-kx^{2}}\,,

(10)

where
k\in\mathbb{R}
is the curvature of the Universe and
k^{-1/2}

has dimension of length.
Note that spatial homogeneity and isotropy allow us to reduce
the ten independent components
of
g_{\mu\nu}
to the scale factor
a
and the curvature
k
.
Based on CMB observations, the curvature
is very close to zero
Ade and others (2014)
.
We will show in Sec.
2.6
that the curvature contribution to the
total energy of the Universe reduces at earlier times, thus justifying
to neglect it in the early Universe.
Then, the line element can be expressed as

{\rm\,d}s^{2}=-m({\rm\,d}t^{2}-a^{2}{\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}^{2})=-m\,a^{2}({\rm\,d}\tau^{2}-{\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}^{2})\,,

(11)

where
\tau
is the conformal time, defined such that

a{\rm\,d}\tau={\rm\,d}t
, and
a\,x^{i}=r^{i}
relates the comoving,
x^{i}
, and physical,
r^{i}
, spatial coordinates.
The physical velocity of an object in an FLRW Universe is

u^{i}_{\rm phys}\equiv\frac{{\rm\,d}r^{i}(t)}{{\rm\,d}t}=a\frac{{\rm\,d}x^{i}(t)}{{\rm\,d}t}+r^{i}H=\frac{{\rm\,d}x^{i}(\tau)}{{\rm\,d}\tau}+x^{i}\,{\cal H}\,,

(12)

where

x^{i}(\tau)\equiv x^{i}\bigl(t(\tau)\bigr)
are the comoving coordinates expressed in terms of conformal time,

H\equiv\dot{a}/a
and
{\cal H}=a^{\prime}/a
are the Hubble and conformal Hubble rates of expansion of the Universe.
The first term is the peculiar velocity,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=a\dot{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}}{}(t)=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}^{\prime}(\tau)
,
measured by a comoving
observer, and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}H=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}{\cal H}
is the Hubble flow.
We note that the velocities are described taking the time
derivative of the positions
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}(\tau)
or
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}(t)
describing the trajectory of the observer.
In general, a dot is used to denote derivatives with respect to
cosmic time and a prime for derivatives with respect to conformal time
in the following.

Then, the FLRW metric tensor and its inverse when one chooses
X^{0}=t

are

g_{\mu\nu}=m\,{\rm diag}\{-1,a^{2},a^{2},a^{2}\}\,,\qquad g^{\mu\nu}=m\,{\rm diag}\{-1,a^{-2},a^{-2},a^{-2}\}\,,

(13)

with determinant
g={\rm det}\,g_{\mu\nu}=-a^{6}
.
We can use
g_{\mu\nu}
to lower indices, such that
X_{\mu}=g_{\mu\nu}X^{\nu}=m(-t,a^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})
.
The non-vanishing Christoffel symbols in the Levi-Civita connection
are

\Gamma^{0}_{ij}=a^{2}\,H\delta_{ij}=a\,{\cal H}\delta_{ij}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{i}_{0j}=H\delta^{i}_{j}=\frac{{\cal H}}{a}\delta^{i}_{j}\,.

(14)

As a reminder, the connection coefficients can be computed from the
metric tensor as

\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\sigma}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,g^{\mu\lambda}(\partial_{\sigma}g_{\lambda\nu}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\lambda\sigma}-\partial_{\lambda}g_{\nu\sigma})\,.

(15)

2.2
Conformal time as
X^{0}

Alternatively, one can consider conformal time as the
X^{0}
variable
(this will be the usual choice in the following sections),
such that
X^{\mu}=(\tau,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})
,
X_{\mu}=m\,a^{2}(-\tau,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{})
,
and the metric tensor components are

g_{\mu\nu}=a^{2}\,m\,{\rm diag}\{-1,1,1,1\}\,=a^{2}\,\eta_{\mu\nu}\,,\qquad g^{\mu\nu}=a^{-2}\eta^{\mu\nu}\,.

(16)

Using conformal time as
X^{0}
, the non-zero Christoffel symbols are

\Gamma^{0}_{00}={\cal H}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{0}_{ij}={\cal H}\,\delta_{ij}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{i}_{0j}={\cal H}\,\delta^{i}_{j}\,.

(17)

One can indistinguishably use cosmic or conformal time
as
X^{0}
to find the
conservation laws.
However, note that the metric and connection tensor components depend on this choice
and, hence, it is crucial to be consistent
with modifications of the choice of geometric variables.

Equation (
16
) shows explicitly that
g_{\mu\nu}
under FLRW geometry
can be expressed as a Weyl transformation
of the Minkowski metric tensor.
This property will be crucial to show how conservation laws are invariant
under conformal transformations in Sec.
3

for a radiation-dominated
perfect fluid, in Sec.
5
for electromagnetism, and in Sec.
6
for MHD.

2.3
Generic
\alpha
-time
\tau_{\alpha}
as
X^{0}

In specific situations,
it might be useful to allow for a generic definition of the
time variable, an
\alpha
-time, as
used, for example, in
{\cal C}
osmo
{\cal L}
attice
Figueroa et al. (2021)
;
Figueroa et al. (2023)
,
which is defined such that
a^{\alpha}{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}={\rm\,d}t
.
The
\alpha
-time reduces to cosmic and conformal times when
\alpha=0
and
1
,
respectively.
For the choice

X^{0}=\tau_{\alpha}
,

g^{00}=-m\,a^{-2\alpha}
and
g_{00}=-m\,a^{2\alpha}
,
and
the line element is

{\rm\,d}s^{2}=-m(a^{2\alpha}{\rm\,d}\tau^{2}_{\alpha}-a^{2}{\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}^{2})\,.

(18)

In the present work, the equations of motion will be described in a
generic
\alpha
-time.
However, we will keep
X^{0}=\tau
in most of the text and will then
transform the equations of motion to the generic
\tau_{\alpha}
.
Of course, the different choices of
X^{0}

do not affect the resulting equations of motion.
When one chooses
X^{0}=\tau_{\alpha}
, the non-zero
components of the Christoffel symbols are

\Gamma^{0}_{00}=\alpha{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\Gamma_{ij}^{0}=a^{-2(\alpha-1)}{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,\delta_{ij}\,,\qquad\Gamma^{i}_{0j}={\cal H}_{\alpha}\,\delta^{i}_{j}\,,

(19)

where

{\cal H}_{\alpha}=(\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}a)/a=a^{\alpha-1}{\cal H}
.
Note that our
{\cal H}_{\alpha}
is denoted by
{\cal H}
in
Figueroa et al. (2021)
, while we restrict
{\cal H}\equiv a^{\prime}/a
to the conformal
Hubble rate in this work.

2.4
Four-velocity

The four-velocity of a particle following a path
X^{\mu}(s)
is

U^{\mu}=\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}(\tilde{s})}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}}\,,

(20)

where
\tilde{s}
is the proper time, such that

{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}^{2}=-m{\rm\,d}s^{2}
.
To find its exact form in units of the peculiar velocity
u^{i}\equiv{\rm\,d}x^{i}(\tau)/{\rm\,d}\tau
,
where
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}(\tau)
describes the trajectory of the fluid particles,
we note that the proper time
can be expressed in the following way

{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}^{2}=-m\,g_{{\mu\nu}}{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}{\rm\,d}X^{\nu}=a^{2}({\rm\,d}\tau^{2}-{\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}^{2})=\,\frac{a^{2}{\rm\,d}\tau^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\,,

(21)

where

\gamma=(1-u^{2})^{-1/2}
is
the usual Lorentz factor in special relativity.
Hence, the four-velocity can be expressed as

U^{\mu}=\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}(\tilde{s})}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}}=\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}(\tau)}{{\rm\,d}\tau}\frac{{\rm\,d}\tau}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}}=\gamma(1,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{})/a\,,

(22)

where

X^{\mu}(\tau)\equiv X^{\mu}\bigl(\tilde{s}(\tau)\bigr)
and

X^{0}=\tau
is chosen to define the four-velocity.
For a generic choice of coordinates, one can express the
four-velocity as

U^{\mu}=\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}(\tilde{s})}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}}=\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}(\tau_{\alpha})}{{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}}\frac{{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}}{{\rm\,d}t}\frac{{\rm\,d}t}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}}=\gamma\,a^{-\alpha}\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}(\tau_{\alpha})}{{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}}\,,

(23)

with
X^{\mu}(\tau_{\alpha})\equiv X^{\mu}\bigl(\tilde{s}(\tau_{\alpha})\bigr)
, and define a velocity field
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}_{\alpha}={\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{\alpha}(\tau_{\alpha})/{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}
, with
a^{\alpha}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{\alpha}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle r$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle r$}}{}
,
where
\alpha=0
yields the physical spatial coordinates
r^{i}

and the physical velocity
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}_{\rm phys}
[cf. Eq. (
12
)].
However, such a choice of the velocity
would require a redefinition of the Lorentz factor in FLRW as the
peculiar velocity
\textstyle u
becomes

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}_{\alpha}+(\alpha-1)\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{\alpha}\,.

(24)

Therefore, only the choice
\alpha=1
to define
\textstyle u
allows
to maintain the special relativity definition of the Lorentz factor.
For this reason, we will use this convention and the four-velocity
components given in Eq. (
22
) in the following sections.
If one alternatively chooses a different
X^{0}=\tau_{\alpha}
, the four-velocity is expressed
as
U^{\mu}=\gamma\,(a^{-\alpha},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}/a)
.
In particular, for
X^{0}=t
,
the four-velocity is
U^{\mu}=\gamma(1,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}/a)
, which has been used
in previous work (cf.
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
).

Noting that the line element can be expressed as

{\rm\,d}s^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}{\rm\,d}X^{\nu}={\rm\,d}X^{\mu}{\rm\,d}X_{\mu}\,,

(25)

U^{\mu}
is required to satisfy the normalization condition,

U^{\mu}U_{\mu}=-m\,\frac{{\rm\,d}X^{\mu}{\rm\,d}X_{\mu}}{\!{\rm\,d}s^{2}}=-m\,.

(26)

The four-acceleration
a^{\mu}
is computed by parallel transport of the derivative of the
four-velocity along itself,

a^{\mu}=U^{\nu}U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\nu}}\,,

(27)

where
the subscript
;\nu
indicates the gravitational covariant derivative (see Sec.
2.5
).
Particles in free fall follow trajectories that are determined by
a^{\mu}=0
(in analogy
to no acceleration in the absence of forces in Newtonian physics), yielding the geodesic equation

\frac{{\rm\,d}U^{\mu}}{{\rm\,d}\tilde{s}}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}U^{\nu}U^{\lambda}=0\,.

(28)

2.5
Covariant derivative

The covariant derivative has been introduced to define the four-acceleration, which can be obtained by parallel transport of a vector
along the curvature of space-time.
The reader is referred to excellent textbooks on general relativity
that are available in the literature
for a
geometrical description of covariant derivatives and general relativity
Weinberg (1972)
;
Baumann (2022)
;
Misner et al. (1973)
;
Wald (1984)
;
Schutz (1985)
;
Carroll (2019)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.
We restrict this section to simply provide useful expressions
of the covariant derivative that will be used to find the conservation
laws in the following sections:

•

For a scalar field
\phi
,

\phi{{}_{\,;\,\mu}}=\partial_{\mu}\phi\,.

(29)

•

For a rank-1 tensor
U^{\nu}
,

U^{\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=\partial_{\mu}U^{\nu}+U^{\lambda}\Gamma^{\nu}_{\lambda\mu}\,,

(30)

where its divergence can be expressed as
U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\,U^{\mu})
.

•

For a rank-2 tensor
T^{\mu\nu}
,

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=

\displaystyle\,\partial_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\mu\sigma}T^{\sigma\nu}+\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\sigma}T^{\mu\sigma}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\,T^{\mu\nu})+\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\sigma}T^{\mu\sigma}\,.

(31)

For the FLRW metric tensor with no curvature,
the Christoffel symbols are given in Eq. (
17
)
using conformal time as the
X^{0}
coordinate.
We observe from Eq. (
31
) that, for an antisymmetric tensor
F^{\mu\nu}
(for
example, the Faraday tensor), the term
\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\sigma}F^{\mu\sigma}
vanishes due to the symmetry of the connection coefficients in the
lower indices.

2.6
Friedmann equations

The dynamics of the metric tensor and the stress-energy distribution is determined
by the Einstein field equation in general relativity,

G_{\mu\nu}+\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}=M_{\rm pl}^{-2}\,T_{\mu\nu}\,,

(32)

where the
reduced Planck mass is
M_{\rm pl}=(8\pi G)^{-1/2}\simeq 2.4\times 10^{18}
GeV.
The cosmological constant
\Lambda
can alternatively be
included as a vacuum energy density
\rho_{\rm vac}
contributing to
the stress-energy tensor
T_{\mu\nu}\to T_{\mu\nu}-\Lambda g_{\mu\nu}M_{\rm pl}^{2}
and then consider

G_{\mu\nu}=M_{\rm pl}^{-2}T_{\mu\nu}\,.

(33)

Let us start focusing on the Einstein tensor,

G_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle{1\over 2}}Rg_{\mu\nu}\,,

(34)

where
R_{\mu\nu}
is the Ricci tensor

R_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{{\mu\nu}}-\partial_{\nu}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\lambda}+\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\lambda\rho}\Gamma^{\rho}_{{\mu\nu}}-\Gamma^{\rho}_{\mu\lambda}\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\nu\rho}\,,

(35)

and its trace
R=R^{\mu}_{\ \mu}
is the Ricci scalar.
From this expression, and using the Christoffel symbols of the FLRW
metric tensor (cf. Eq. (
14
) or Eq. (
17
) for
X^{0}=t
or
\tau
), one finds the non-zero components of the Ricci tensor,

\displaystyle R^{0}_{\ \,0}=\,

\displaystyle\,3m\,\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}=\frac{3m}{a^{2}}\biggl(\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}-{\cal H}^{2}\biggr)\,,

(36a)

\displaystyle R^{i}_{\ \,j}=\,

\displaystyle\,m\,\biggl(\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}+2H^{2}+2\frac{k}{a^{2}}\biggr)\delta^{i}_{j}=\frac{m}{a^{2}}\biggl(\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}+{\cal H}^{2}+2k\biggr)\delta^{i}_{j}\,,

(36b)

where we have recovered the curvature
k
in the metric tensor
(cf.
Weinberg (1972)
;
Kolb and Turner (1990)
;
Baumann (2022)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
for details).
The Ricci scalar is

R=R^{0}_{\ 0}+R^{i}_{\ i}=6m\,\biggl(\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}+H^{2}+\frac{k}{a^{2}}\biggr)=\frac{6m}{a^{2}}\,\biggl(\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}+k\biggr)\,.

(37)

We note that

R_{ij}\propto g_{ij}
and
R_{i0}=0
are expected based on homogeneity and isotropy.
The non-zero terms of the Einstein tensor are

\displaystyle G^{0}_{\ 0}=

\displaystyle-3m\biggl(H^{2}+\frac{k}{a^{2}}\biggr)=-\frac{3m}{a^{2}}({\cal H}^{2}+k)\,,

(38a)

\displaystyle G^{i}_{\ j}=

\displaystyle-m\biggl(2\frac{\ddot{a}}{a}+H^{2}+\frac{k}{a^{2}}\biggr)\delta^{i}_{j}=-\frac{m}{a^{2}}\biggl(2\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}-{\cal H}^{2}+k\biggr)\delta^{i}_{j}\,.

(38b)

Finally, the stress-energy tensor of the Universe can be obtained taking into account homogeneity
and isotropy at large scales.
Hence,
T_{0i}=0
, and
T_{ij}=\bar{p}\,g_{ij}

is the isotropic pressure tensor.
Furthermore,
T_{00}=\bar{\rho}\,g_{00}
is the background energy density.
In the reference frame of the Hubble observer
U^{\mu}=(1,{\boldsymbol{0}})/a
, the covariant
stress-energy tensor is

T^{\mu}_{\ \ \nu}=(\bar{p}+\bar{\rho})\,U^{\mu}U_{\nu}+m\,\bar{p}\,g^{\mu}_{\ \,\nu}=m\,{\rm diag}\{-\bar{\rho},\bar{p},\bar{p},\bar{p}\}\,.

(39)

This stress-energy tensor corresponds to the one for a perfect fluid
4
4

4

A perfect fluid is a fluid composed by particles in
local thermal equilibrium described, for classical subrelativistic systems, by a Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution
function
f_{0}
(see Sec.
4
), and it is the required description to satisfy the homogeneity
and isotropy of the Universe at large scales.
with
\bar{p}
and
\bar{\rho}

denoting the average pressure and energy density in the Universe as a function of time, which
will be determined by the particle content at each time.
For an equation of state with a constant ratio of pressure to
density,
\bar{p}=w\bar{\rho}
,
the Universe can be considered to be dominated by massive dust particles
(matter domination) with
w=0
, by massless particles (radiation domination) with

w={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
, or by dark energy with
w=-1
, which is equivalent to introducing a cosmological
constant
\Lambda
.
A dark energy contribution is necessary to explain the accelerated rate of expansion of the Universe observed
at present time, corresponding
to a homogeneous, negative pressure
in the stress-energy tensor

T^{\mu\nu}=-m\,\rho_{\rm vac}\,g^{\mu\nu}
.
A vacuum energy has been estimated in quantum field theory (QFT), however,
its estimate differs by
many orders of magnitude with respect to
the observed critical energy density of the Universe at present time,

\rho_{\rm vac}^{\rm QFT}/\rho_{\rm crit}^{0}\sim 10^{56}

Weinberg (2008)
.
Hence, the origin of dark energy is still an unsolved problem.

Einstein field equations automatically imply the conservation of the stress-energy tensor as

G^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
and, hence,

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0\,.

(40)

These conservation laws are consistent with the relativistic Boltzmann
equation, which describes the classical conservation of the distribution
function of particles
f
in a fluid.
They can be obtained from the first moment of the Boltzmann equation
(cf.
Cercignani and Kremer (2002)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
for details).
For the perfect fluid description and a comoving observer
U^{\mu}=(1,{\boldsymbol{0}})/a
,
the energy equation is found from the temporal component,
T^{\mu 0}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,

\partial_{\tau}\bar{\rho}+3\,(1+w)\,{\cal H}\,\bar{\rho}=0\,.

(41)

The resulting continuity
equation is of first-order, hence we can transform
\partial_{\tau}\to\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}
and
{\cal H}\to{\cal H}_{\alpha}
to describe it for any generic

\alpha
-time.
This equation can be integrated to find

\int\frac{{\rm\,d}\bar{\rho}}{\bar{\rho}}=-3\int\frac{{\rm\,d}a}{a}(1+w)\,,

(42)

which, for a constant
w
, gives a solution

\bar{\rho}\sim a^{-3(1+w)}
.
In particular,

\bar{\rho}\sim a^{-3}
,
a^{-4}
,
a^{0}
for matter, radiation, and vacuum dominations.
We note that the introduction of a cosmological constant does not modify the energy equation
as
(\Lambda g^{\mu\nu}){{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
.

Finally, Friedmann equations are obtained introducing Eqs. (
38
) and (
39
) into Eq. (
32
),

{\cal H}^{2}=a^{2}\frac{\bar{\rho}}{3M_{\rm pl}^{2}}-k\,,\qquad\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}=\frac{a^{2}}{6M_{\rm pl}^{2}}(\bar{\rho}-3\bar{p})-k\,.

(43)

Note that the curvature can be absorbed in the total energy density defining
\rho_{k}=-3M_{\rm pl}^{2}k/a^{2}
,

{\cal H}^{2}=\frac{a^{2}}{3M_{\rm pl}^{2}}\bar{\rho}\,,\qquad\frac{a^{\prime\prime}}{a}=\frac{a^{2}}{6M_{\rm pl}^{2}}(\bar{\rho}-3\bar{p})\,.

(44)

These equations allow us to evolve the scale factor
a
as a function of
\tau
once the
background values
\bar{\rho}
and
\bar{p}
are known.
Each of the energy contributions can be normalized to the
present-day critical energy density
\rho_{\rm crit}=3M_{\rm pl}^{2}H_{0}^{2}
, corresponding
to a closed universe with
k=0
,

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm rad}=\frac{\rho_{\rm rad}}{\rho_{\rm crit}}=\Omega_{r,0}\,(a/a_{0})^{-4}\,,\qquad\Omega_{\rm mat}=\Omega_{m,0}\,(a/a_{0})^{-3}\,,\qquad\Omega_{k}=\Omega_{k,0}\,(a/a_{0})^{-2}\,,

(45)

where the values at present time have been inferred from CMB
observations as described by the
\Lambda
CDM
model (with a cosmological constant
\Lambda
describing
dark energy, cold dark matter, CDM,
and baryons contributing to the matter content of the Universe)
Ade and others (2014)
;
Ade and others (2015)
;
Aghanim and others (2020)
,

\Omega_{r,0}\simeq 9.4\times 10^{-5}\,,\qquad\Omega_{m,0}\simeq 0.32\,,\qquad|\Omega_{k,0}|\leq 0.01\,,\qquad\Omega_{\Lambda}\simeq 0.68\,.

(46)

Then, the Hubble rate as a function of the scale factor
a
can be expressed as a ratio to its value at present time,

\frac{H^{2}(a)}{H_{0}^{2}}=\Omega_{r,0}\,(a/a_{0})^{-4}+\Omega_{m,0}\,(a/a_{0})^{-3}+\Omega_{k,0}\,(a/a_{0})^{-2}+\Omega_{\Lambda}=\Omega(a)\,.

(47)

As curvature decreases slower than matter with a ratio
\Omega_{k}/\Omega_{m}\sim a/a_{0}
, the relevance of curvature in the total energy budget
decreases at earlier times.
Hence, it is safe to set
\Omega_{k}=0
in the early Universe.
We note that going back in time, we find an era of matter domination before
\Lambda
would become
the dominant contribution, and an even earlier period of radiation domination, since radiation
decreases faster than matter, prior to matter-radiation equality.
The latter corresponds to the era of the Universe history that we
focus on in the present work.

Assuming that at any time, the Universe is dominated by a single component,
Eq. (
43
) can be solved to find the evolution of the scale factor with conformal time as

a\sim\tau^{2/(1+3w)}
for
w\neq-{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.
In particular,

a\sim\tau^{2}
,
\tau
, and
-1/\tau
for matter, radiation, and vacuum
5
5

5

The evolution of the scale factor when
w=-1
is
a_{0}^{-1}-a^{-1}=\sqrt{\bar{\rho}/3}\,(\tau-\tau_{0})\,M_{\rm pl}^{-1}
.
(dark-energy) dominations.
In terms of cosmic time, we find
a\sim t^{2/[3(1+w)]}
for
w\neq-1
,
i.e.,

a\sim t^{2/3}
and
t^{1/2}
for
w=0
and
{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
respectively,
and
a\sim e^{Ht}
for
w=-1
.
The exponential expansion
in vacuum domination corresponds to a de Sitter Universe,
observed at present time, and considered during
the period of inflation in the early Universe.

Considering both matter and radiation near equality (eq), such that
\bar{\rho}=\bar{\rho}_{m}+\bar{\rho}_{r}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\bar{\rho}_{\rm eq}[(a_{\rm eq}/a)^{3}+(a_{\rm eq}/a)^{4}]
, we can find a solution

a(\tau)=a_{\rm eq}\bigl[(\tau/\tau_{\ast})^{2}+2(\tau/\tau_{\ast})\bigr]\,,\qquad\tau_{\ast}=\frac{\tau_{\rm eq}}{\sqrt{2}-1}\,,

(48)

valid at epochs when mass and radiation contributions are almost equal, like, for example,
around the epoch of recombination,
a_{\rm rec}\sim 9\times 10^{-4}
, which is close to
a_{\rm eq}\sim 3\times 10^{-4}
.

3
Relativistic fluid dynamics in an expanding Universe

In this section, the equations of motion describing
the energy density
\rho
and the velocity field
\textstyle u

in an expanding background are computed for perfect fluids, i.e.,
fluids that are in local thermal equilibrium (LTE).
Deviations with respect to LTE will be considered
in Sec.
4

to include viscous effects and heat fluxes,
corresponding to the first-order description of imperfect fluids.
In first-order fluid dynamics,
the viscous terms
follow a Navier-Stokes description and heat fluxes correspond to
those described by Fourier’s law of thermal conduction.
As both corrections lead to acausal fluids, they are not a satisfactory
theory for relativistic imperfect fluids.
We briefly discuss this issue in Sec.
4
and refer the reader to
the review
Romatschke (2010)
and the textbook
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
for more extensive descriptions.
The conservation laws for charged fluids in presence of electromagnetic
fields are described in Sec.
6
, leading
to the equations of motion that, combined with Maxwell equations (see Sec.
5
), describe magnetohydrodynamics (MHD).

Simulations of the early Universe describing the dynamics
of a radiation-dominated fluid
have been performed in the subrelativistic regime,
following the pioneer work of
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
, in the
context of decaying MHD turbulence (e.g.,
Brandenburg et al. (2017a)
),
production of GWs (e.g.,
Roper Pol et al. (2020b)
), or chiral decaying MHD (e.g.,
Schober et al. (2018)
).
For a more comprehensive literature, see references in the introduction.
In their work, the equations of motion for a perfect fluid are found from the conservation
of the stress-energy tensor,

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
after setting
\gamma^{2}\to 1
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}=-

\displaystyle\,\frac{4}{3}\bigl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\bigr]\,,

(49a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{3}\bigl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\bigr]-\frac{1}{4}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,,

(49b)

where
\tilde{\rho}=a^{4}\rho
is the comoving energy density, and
the comoving pressure is described using the ultrarelativistic
equation of state
\tilde{p}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{\rho}
with
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
(see Sec.
3.1
).
In this case, the fluid equations of motion are conformally invariant
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
(see Sec.
3.3
).
In the following, we will present
the derivation of these equations for a generic constant
equation of state
and will extend them
to include relativistic effects, as already presented in Eqs. (
2
).
Furthermore, we will show in Secs.
3.4
and
3.5
[see, in particular, Eqs. (
100
)]
that these equations are required
to be corrected (by the terms indicated below in red) to:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}=-

\displaystyle\,\frac{4}{3}\bigl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bf{\textstyle{1\over 2}}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\bigr]\,,

(50a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{3}\bigl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\bf{\textstyle{1\over 2}}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\bigr]-\frac{1}{4}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,,

(50b)

due to the fact that the term

\partial_{\tau}u^{2}
presents non-negligible contributions in the
subrelativistic limit, which had been previously
ignored, as a consequence of setting
\gamma^{2}\to 1
.
Indeed, note that
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}=\gamma^{4}\partial_{\tau}u^{2}
is related to the conservation
of the kinetic energy contribution to the energy density
T^{00}=\rho+\rho_{\rm kin}
,
where
\rho_{\rm kin}=(p+\rho)\gamma^{2}u^{2}
(see Sec.
3.2
).
Therefore, the time derivative of
\rho_{\rm kin}
,
\partial_{\tau}\rho_{\rm kin}=\gamma^{2}(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})(u^{2}\partial_{\tau}\rho+\gamma^{2}\rho\,\partial_{\tau}u^{2})
balances
with the power exerted by the forces to the fluid, e.g.,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}p
.
These forces include terms of leading order
also in the subrelativistic limit when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)

that lead to the corrections in Eqs. (
50
).

3.1
Equation of state

We are in general interested in the situations when the Universe
is dominated by radiation, such that
c_{\rm s}^{2}\approx{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.
In general, the energy density can be decomposed into the energy
density of massive (
\rho_{\rm mat}
) and massless radiation (
\rho_{\rm rad}
) particles,

\rho=\rho_{\rm mat}+\rho_{\rm rad}=\rho_{m}(1+\varepsilon)+\rho_{\rm rad}\,,

(51)

where
\rho_{m}
is the rest-mass density
and
\rho_{m}\,\varepsilon
is
the internal energy density.

When
\rho_{\rm mat}\ll\rho_{\rm rad}
, then the fluid is dominated by
radiation
\rho\simeq\rho_{\rm rad}
, and the pressure is described by the ultrarelativistic equation
of state
p_{\rm rad}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\rho_{\rm rad}\simeq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}\rho_{\rm rad}
.
When massive particles are present but still the contribution to the pressure is dominated by radiation
particles, we model the plasma with an equation of
state described by a constant
c_{\rm s}^{2}
, such that
the total pressure is
p=c_{\rm s}^{2}\rho
, i.e.,

p=p_{\rm mat}+p_{\rm rad}\simeq p_{\rm rad}=c_{\rm s}^{2}(\rho_{\rm mat}+\rho_{\rm rad})={\textstyle{1\over 3}}\rho_{\rm rad}\,.

(52)

In this case, one can approximate the squared
speed of sound as

3c_{\rm s}^{2}=\frac{1}{1+\rho_{\rm mat}/\rho_{\rm rad}}\,.

(53)

For such an equation of state, and under the assumption that the
particles in the fluid are coupled, such that the
fluid can be described with a common four-velocity
U^{\mu}
(for interacting multifluid
systems, see
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
and references therein),
the system of momentum and energy
conservation equations,
T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,
is closed.
In this work, we will restrict our MHD description to this type of systems: fluids
with radiation particles corresponding to the dominant contribution to the
pressure, such that
p/\rho
is a constant
c_{\rm s}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)
.

On the other hand, when
\rho_{\rm rad}\ll\rho_{\rm mat}
, the fluid is
dominated by massive particles, and
p\ll\rho
.
In this case, the ideal gas equation of state relates
the pressure and the internal energy density,
p=c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\rho_{m}\,\varepsilon
with a constant
c_{\rm s}^{2}
when
one ignores entropy variations in LTE.
Therefore, as the equation of state does not directly relate

p
to the total
\rho=\rho_{m}(1+\varepsilon)
,
the system obtained from

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
is no longer closed.
Hence,
it is needed to introduce the
rest-mass conservation law for coupled fluids,

{J^{\mu}_{\rm mat}}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,
with
J^{\mu}_{\rm mat}=\rho_{m}U^{\mu}
.
In the subrelativistic regime and ignoring the expansion of the
Universe, the system of equations would reduce to
the usual Newtonian limit of mass, momentum, and energy conservation
(Euler equations)

\displaystyle D_{t}\rho_{m}+\rho_{m}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=0\,,\qquad\rho_{m}D_{t}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}p=0\,,\qquad\rho_{m}D_{t}\varepsilon+p\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=0\,.

(54)

In the following, we will always assume that the pressure and total
energy density can be described by an equation of state
p=c_{\rm s}^{2}\rho
,
i.e., that the radiation pressure is the dominant
contribution to the total one, such that
the
T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0

conservation laws
already represent a closed system.
Furthermore, we will assume that the fluid perturbations are smaller
than the background, such that they do not feedback on the metric
tensor.
Hence, we will consider the fluid perturbations over the FLRW
