background metric tensor described in the previous section.
For generality, we will
allow the background energy density and
pressure,
\bar{\rho}
and
\bar{p}
, to
contain contributions in addition to those from the fluid,
\rho

and
p
, such that the background constant describing the equation of state,
w
,
does not necessarily need to be equal to
c_{\rm s}^{2}
(see Sec.
2.6
).

3.2
Stress-energy tensor of a perfect fluid

The stress-energy tensor of a perfect fluid can be described as

T^{\mu\nu}=(p+\rho)\,U^{\mu}U^{\nu}+pg^{\mu\nu}\,.

(55a)

In the following, we consider the signature

m=1
and
X^{0}=\tau
for compactness
as this choice does not affect the equations of motion.
The stress-energy tensor can also be expressed in terms
of the projection tensor
h^{\mu\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}+U^{\mu}U^{\nu}
,

T^{\mu\nu}=\rho\,U^{\mu}U^{\nu}+ph^{\mu\nu}\,,

(56)

where
h^{\mu\nu}
satisfies
h^{\mu\nu}U_{\nu}=0
.
The different components of the stress-energy tensor are:

a^{2}T^{00}=(p+\rho)\,\gamma^{2}-p\,,\qquad a^{2}T^{0i}=(p+\rho)\,\gamma^{2}\,u^{i}\,,\qquad a^{2}T^{ij}=(p+\rho)\,\gamma^{2}\,u^{i}u^{j}+p\delta^{ij}\,.

(57)

Note that, in the following,
we do not distinguish between
u^{i}
and
u_{i}
, as
\textstyle u
corresponds
to the peculiar velocity, and not to the spatial components of the
four-velocity.
The covariant
energy density in the Hubble observer frame can be rearranged to

-T^{0}_{\ \,0}=(p+\rho)\,\gamma^{2}u^{2}+\rho=\rho_{\rm kin}+\rho\,,

(58)

using the identity
\gamma^{2}u^{2}=\gamma^{2}-1
,
where
\rho_{\rm kin}=(p+\rho)\gamma^{2}u^{2}
is the relativistic kinetic
energy density.

The trace of the stress-energy tensor plays an important role in the
conservation laws, as we will see in the following.
We first express the trace of the spatial components as

T^{i}_{\ \,i}=a^{2}T^{ij}\delta_{ij}=\rho_{\rm kin}+3p\,,

(59)

such that the trace of the stress-energy tensor becomes

T=T^{\mu}_{\ \mu}=T^{0}_{\ 0}+T^{i}_{\ i}=3p-\rho\,.

(60)

Then, for a radiation-dominated fluid,
p={\textstyle{1\over 3}}\rho
,
the trace of the stress-energy tensor vanishes.
This condition implies that the
equations of motion are conformally invariant,
i.e., the equations in an expanding background reduce to those in flat Minkowski
space-time after a conformal transformation
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
, as
we show in the following section.

3.3
Conservation laws of a perfect fluid

The conservation laws are obtained from Eq. (
40
) as a consequence of Bianchi identities

G^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\,T^{\mu\nu})+\Gamma^{\nu}_{\mu\sigma}T^{\mu\sigma}=0\,,

(61)

where
;\mu
is the gravitational covariant derivative given in Eq. (
31
),

\partial_{\mu}
the partial
derivative,
\sqrt{-g}=a^{4}
for the FLRW metric tensor using
X^{0}=\tau
, and
\Gamma^{\nu}_{{\mu\sigma}}
are the FLRW Christoffel symbols, given in
Eq. (
17
).
The conservation laws correspond to the conservation of energy
and momentum and can be found setting
\mu=0
and
i
, respectively, in Eq. (
61
).
They characterize
a closed system of equations (for the energy density
\rho
and the
peculiar velocity
\textstyle u
) when the equation of state relating the
total energy density and the pressure is known, e.g.,
p=c_{\rm s}^{2}\rho
.
This allows us to study coupled fluids composed by radiation and
massive particles when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)
is a known constant.
Alternatively, an equivalent set of the equations of
motion can be found by projecting

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
in the parallel,
U_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,
and perpendicular directions to
U_{\nu}
,
h_{\nu\lambda}T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,
leading to the energy and momentum equations, respectively
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.

3.3.1 Conformal invariance and conservation laws

An important aspect of the conservation laws of perfect fluids
is that they are conformally invariant when the trace of the
stress-energy tensor vanishes
Liang (1977)
;
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
.
To show this result, let us consider two metrics that are related by a conformal transformation as

g^{{\mu\nu}}=\Omega^{2}(x^{\mu})\,\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
.
This corresponds, for example, to the FLRW metric
tensor
g^{\mu\nu}
when
we use conformal time as the time coordinate, where

\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}
would correspond to flat Minkowskian space-time and
\Omega=a^{-1}(\tau)
is the inverse scale factor.
The transformation of the covariant derivative of a symmetric
T^{\mu\nu}
in a metric tensor

g^{\mu\nu}
is

\displaystyle T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{-g}\,T^{\mu\nu})+\Gamma^{\nu}_{{\mu\sigma}}T^{\mu\sigma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\Omega^{4}}{\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}}\partial_{\mu}(\sqrt{-\tilde{g}}\,\Omega^{-4}\,T^{\mu\nu})+\tilde{\Gamma}^{\nu}_{{\mu\sigma}}T^{\mu\sigma}-2\,T^{{\mu\nu}}\partial_{\mu}\ln\Omega+T^{\mu}_{\ \mu}\,\partial^{\nu}\ln\Omega\,,

(62)

where
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\nu}_{{\mu\sigma}}
are the Christoffel symbols
of the metric tensor
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
.
Introducing the comoving stress-energy tensor
\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}=\Omega^{-6}\,T^{\mu\nu}
, we find

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=\Omega^{6}\bigl(\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\tilde{\mu}}}+\tilde{T}^{\sigma}_{\ \,\sigma}\,\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\ln\Omega\bigr)=0\Leftrightarrow\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\tilde{\mu}}}+\tilde{T}^{\sigma}_{\ \,\sigma}\,\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\ln\Omega=0\,,

(63)

where
;\tilde{\mu}

is the covariant derivative in the
metric tensor
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
.

As we have shown in Eq. (
60
), the stress-energy tensor of a perfect fluid is traceless when
p={\textstyle{1\over 3}}\rho
.
Then, in this case, the equations of motion become invariant under conformal
transformations,

T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0\Leftrightarrow\tilde{T}^{{\mu\nu}}{{}_{;\,\tilde{\mu}}}=0\,.

(64)

In particular, for a homogeneous and isotropic
expanding Universe,
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=\eta^{\mu\nu}
is the Minkowski
metric tensor, such that the equations of motion are conformally flat,

\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}=0
.
For non-zero trace,
the conservation laws Eq. (
63
) in an expanding background with
\Omega=a^{-1}
become

\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu 0}+\tilde{T}\,{\cal H}=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu i}=0\,.

(65)

The comoving trace
is
\tilde{T}=a^{4}\,T=3\tilde{p}-\tilde{\rho}
,
where we have also defined
\tilde{\rho}=a^{4}\,\rho
and
\tilde{p}=a^{4}\,p
as the comoving energy density and pressure.
Therefore, the energy and momentum equations are
directly found in conformal time using Eq. (
65
),

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{00}+\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{0i}=(\tilde{\rho}-3\tilde{p})\,{\cal H}\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{0i}+\partial_{j}\tilde{T}^{ij}=0\,.

(66)

We notice that this system of equations can be generalized to different geometries in general relativity, following the so-called Valencia formulation (see
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
and references therein),
where the
-\tilde{T}\,{\cal H}
term
in the right-hand side of the energy equation corresponds to the extrinsic curvature

K_{ij}
contracted with the perfect fluid
stress-energy tensor
\tilde{T}^{ij}
in FLRW geometry
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.

Moreover, these equations can be described in a generic
\alpha
-time,
by rescaling
\partial_{\tau}\to a^{1-\alpha}\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}
.
Similarly, we can also rescale
{\cal H}\to a^{1-\alpha}{\cal H}_{\alpha}
with
{\cal H}_{\alpha}=(\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}a)/a
, such that the equations of motion in
\alpha
-time become

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{00}+a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{0i}=(\tilde{\rho}-3\tilde{p})\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{0i}+a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{j}\tilde{T}^{ij}=0\,.

(67)

Note that for cosmic time,
a^{-1}\partial_{j}
corresponds to a derivative
with respect to the physical coordinate
r_{i}=a\,x_{i}
and
{\cal H}/a=H
.
In general, the factor
a^{\alpha-1}
can be absorbed in a
space-coordinate
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}_{\lambda}=a^{-\lambda}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}
with
\lambda=\alpha-1
.
This rescaling is possible and reduces to a conformally flat system of equations
when
p={\textstyle{1\over 3}}\rho
as long as the mapping
g^{\mu\nu}=a^{-2}\eta^{\mu\nu}
is satisfied.

From now on, unless otherwise stated, we will consider
\alpha=1
for compactness of the resulting
equations.
To recover the expression for any
\alpha
-time, it is enough to include
the
a^{\alpha-1}
term in the divergence term

\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{i\mu}

and to substitute
{\cal H}\to{\cal H}_{\alpha}
.

3.3.2 Conservation laws for the physical stress-energy components

Using the comoving stress-energy tensor components,
\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}=a^{6}T^{\mu\nu}
, allows us
to find conformally flat equations of motion for a perfect fluid
when
\rho=3p
and the coordinates
X^{\mu}
are chosen such that
the FLRW metric tensor maps to Minkowski space-time as
g^{\mu\nu}=a^{-2}\eta^{\mu\nu}

(i.e., taking conformal time and comoving space coordinates).
Let us now consider the equations of motion
of the physical covariant
stress-energy components
T^{0}_{\ \,\mu}=T^{0\nu}g_{\mu\nu}=a^{2}\,T^{0\nu}\eta_{\mu\nu}
in an expanding background,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}T^{0}_{\ \,0}\,+

\displaystyle\,a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{i}T^{i}_{\ \,0}=(T^{i}_{\ \,i}-3\,T^{0}_{\ \,0})\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}=f^{H}_{0}\,,

(68a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}T^{0}_{\ \,i}\,+

\displaystyle\,a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{j}T^{j}_{\ \,i}=-4\,T^{0}_{\ \,i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}=f^{H}_{i}\,,

(68b)

where
f^{H}_{\mu}

corresponds to an effective Hubble “force” (Hubble friction) that appears
due to the expansion of the Universe
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
,

f^{H}_{0}=(4\gamma^{2}-1)(p+\rho)\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad f^{H}_{i}=-4\,(p+\rho)\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\gamma^{2}u^{i}\,.

(69)

In the subrelativistic regime, when
\gamma^{2}\simeq 1
, the stress-energy
terms in the trace simplify to
T^{0}_{\ \,0}=-\rho
and
T^{i}_{\ \,i}=3p
,
such that the equations of motion
(
68
)
become

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}(\rho\gamma^{2})+a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{i}\bigl[(p+\rho)u^{i}\bigr]=-

\displaystyle 3\,(p+\rho)\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,

(70a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}\bigl[(p+\rho)\gamma^{2}\,u^{i}\bigr]+a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{j}\bigl[(p+\rho)\,u^{i}u^{j}+p\delta^{ij}\bigr]=-

\displaystyle 4\,(p+\rho)\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,u^{i}\,.

(70b)

We have kept the
\gamma^{2}
terms in the time derivatives
of
T^{0}_{\ \,0}
and
T^{0}_{\ \,i}
because, as anticipated at
the beginning of the section,
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}

leads to additional subrelativistic terms for a generic value
of
p/\rho=c_{\rm s}^{2}

that are only negligible for dust, when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
.

3.3.3 Generic scaling and super-comoving coordinates

Let us now consider a generic scaling of the fluid variables,

\tilde{p}=a^{\beta}p\,,\qquad\tilde{\rho}=a^{\beta}\rho\,,\qquad\tilde{u}^{i}=a^{\delta}u^{i}\,,

(71)

such that
\beta=4
and
\delta=0
recover the comoving variables
used in Eq. (
66
).
The rescaled stress-energy components include the scaling
of the fluid variables and an additional
a^{2}
to take into
account the prefactor in Eq. (
57
),

\displaystyle\tilde{T}^{00}=

\displaystyle\,a^{2+\beta}T^{00}=(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\gamma^{2}-\tilde{p}\,,

(72a)

\displaystyle\tilde{T}^{0i}=

\displaystyle\,a^{2+\beta+\delta}T^{0i}=(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\gamma^{2}\,\tilde{u}^{i}\,,

(72b)

\displaystyle\tilde{T}^{ij}=

\displaystyle\,a^{2+\beta+2\delta}\,(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\gamma^{2}\,\tilde{u}^{i}\tilde{u}^{j}+a^{2+\beta}\,\tilde{p}\,\delta^{ij}\,.

(72c)

The energy and momentum conservation
equations for this generic scaling become

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{00}\,+

\displaystyle\,a^{\alpha-1-\delta}\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{0i}=\tilde{f}^{0}_{H}\,,

(73a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{0i}\,+

\displaystyle\,a^{\alpha-1-\delta}\partial_{j}\bigl[(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\gamma^{2}\,\tilde{u}^{i}\tilde{u}^{j}+a^{2\delta}\tilde{p}\,\delta^{ij}\bigr]=\tilde{f}^{i}_{H}\,,

(73b)

where we find Hubble forcing components
6
6

6

The
Hubble forcing terms
\tilde{f}_{H}^{\mu}
resultant from the rescaling cannot be
directly rescaled to the components in Eqs. (
68
), since they also
incorporate the contribution from the time
derivatives of the scale factor when rescaling the
stress-energy tensor components
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
.
proportional to
{\cal H}_{\alpha}

in both the energy and momentum equations,

\tilde{f}^{0}_{H}=-\bigl[(3-\beta+a^{-2\delta}\tilde{u}^{2})(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}+\beta\,\tilde{p}\bigr]\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\tilde{f}^{i}_{H}=-(4-\beta-\delta)\,\tilde{T}^{0i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,.

(74)

Note that these terms reduce to those in Eq. (
69
) when
\beta=\delta=0
, and to those in Eq. (
67
) when
\beta=4
and
\delta=0
.
The latter choice appears naturally when we try to get rid of the Hubble
friction terms.
In first place, to make
\tilde{f}^{0}_{H}
vanish
for a relativistic bulk velocity with
u^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)
,
the only possible choice is
\delta=0
.
Then, we observe that only for
\beta=4
, the dependence on
\gamma^{2}
vanishes.

In the following, we will consider a
Hubble friction
\tilde{f}_{H}^{\mu}
to allow a generic
choice of the rescaling.
However, note that when
\delta\neq 0
, the relation
between the Lorentz factor and the scaled velocity

\tilde{u}^{i}
is not the same as in special relativity,

\gamma^{2}=\frac{1}{1-a^{-2\delta}\tilde{u}^{2}}\,.

(75)

3.3.4 Subrelativistic limit

It seems like
\beta=4
and
\delta=0
is, in general,
the best choice for the scaling of the fluid variables as it allows to obtain
conformally flat equations of motion when the fluid particles are relativistic.
However, when the fluid bulk motion is subrelativistic,
a different scaling to get rid of the Hubble term in the energy
equation,
\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}
, can be found and can be
a better choice in some cases.
Taking the limit
u^{2}\ll 1
in Eq. (
74
), we find

\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}=-\bigl[(3-\beta)\,\tilde{\rho}+3\tilde{p}\bigr]\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}=-(4-\beta-\delta)\,\tilde{T}^{0i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,.

(76)

Taking a constant equation of state
\tilde{p}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{\rho}
,

\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}
vanishes when
\beta=3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})
for any value of
c_{\rm s}^{2}
,
and the remaining Hubble term in the momentum equation,
\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}
,
becomes

\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}=(3c_{\rm s}^{2}+\delta-1)\,\tilde{T}^{0i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,.

(77)

Then, the energy equation becomes conformally flat for
any choice
\alpha=\delta+1
,
which allows us to get rid of the scale-factor-dependent term in Eq. (
73a
).
The energy and momentum equations become

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2})\,+

\displaystyle\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\partial_{i}(\tilde{\rho}u^{i})=0\,,

(78a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}u^{i})\,+

\displaystyle\,\partial_{j}(\tilde{\rho}u^{i}u^{j})+a^{2\delta}\frac{\partial_{i}\tilde{p}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}=(3c_{\rm s}^{2}+\delta-1)\,\tilde{\rho}u^{i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,

(78b)

where we are only left with a dependence on
{\cal H}_{\alpha}
in the momentum equation,
corresponding to a Hubble friction term that will affect the velocity field evolution.

This will be a useful choice when
\tilde{p}\neq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}\tilde{\rho}
in the subrelativistic regime.
Indeed,
this choice minimizes the appearance
of Hubble-dependent terms in the equations of conservation of
energy and momentum due to the fact that a
non-vanishing
\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}
in the energy equation
will propagate to the momentum equation, as we will see in Sec.
3.5
.
We remind the reader that a choice yielding a vanishing
\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}

was not in general possible for relativistic flows.

3.3.5 Super-comoving coordinates when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1

In the previous section, we have shown that
\alpha=\delta+1
is
a useful scaling for subrelativistic bulk flows with
u^{2}\ll 1
.
A particular choice of
\delta=1
and
\alpha=2
(known as super-comoving
coordinates) is common
in cosmological simulations during matter domination as it allows to get rid of the friction terms as originally proposed in
Martel and Shapiro (1998)
,
based on previous work in
Shandarin (1980)
.
An alternative choice for the super-comoving coordinates was proposed
in
Banerjee and Jedamzik (2004)
.
The latter choice adapts the
\alpha
-time to the Universe expansion during
matter domination, setting

\alpha={\textstyle{3\over 2}}
and
\delta={\textstyle{1\over 2}}
, and allows to keep a constant Hubble rate
in
\alpha
-time, being a useful choice to compute the evolution of primordial magnetic
fields across recombination
Jedamzik and Saveliev (2019)
;
Trivedi et al. (2018)
;
Jedamzik and Pogosian (2020)
;
Jedamzik et al. (2025a)
;
Jedamzik et al. (2025b)
.

For the case of a matter-dominated fluid (
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
),
it is possible to recover conformally flat fluid equations
in the subrelativistic regime, following the super-comoving variables choice
proposed in
Martel and Shapiro (1998)
.
We can see from Eq. (
78b
) that the choice
\delta=1-3c_{\rm s}^{2}

allows us to get rid of the Hubble friction. However,
as a consequence of
\delta\neq 0
, an additional dependence with

a
appears in the pressure gradient.
We can also get rid of this term
when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
, since then the enthalpy is dominated by the energy density,

\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho}\approx\tilde{\rho}
in
Eqs. (
72
).
Hence, the only dependence on the pressure
remains in the
\tilde{T}^{ij}
component, such that

\tilde{\rho}
and
\tilde{p}
become decoupled in the equations,
allowing us to separately rescale

\tilde{p}=a^{\chi}\,p\,,\qquad{\rm with}\quad\chi=\beta+2\delta\,.

(79)

Finally, to get rid of the Hubble friction, we can choose
\delta=1
and, hence,
\alpha=2
,

\beta=3
, and
\chi=5
.
The resulting conservation laws then become conformally flat
Martel and Shapiro (1998)
,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2})+\partial_{i}(\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i})=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}\tilde{u}^{i})+\partial_{j}(\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i}\tilde{u}^{j})+\partial_{i}\tilde{p}=0\,.

(80)

We note that the relation between the Lorentz factor and
\tilde{u}^{i}
is not
the same as in Minkowski space-time when
\delta\neq 0
[cf. Eq. (
75
)].
However, as we deal with subrelativistic flows in this limit, this correction
becomes negligible.

An alternative choice of the
super-comoving coordinates is found when one chooses

\alpha={\textstyle{3\over 2}}
and
\delta={\textstyle{1\over 2}}
, which allows to absorb the
Universe expansion in the time coordinate when the Universe
is matter-dominated,
a\sim t^{2/3}
, such that
\ln a\sim\tau_{\alpha}
and,
hence,
{\cal H}_{\alpha}
is constant in
\alpha
-time
Banerjee and Jedamzik (2004)
.
For a generic background equation of state
w\neq-1
, the scale factor
evolves as
a\sim t^{2/[3(1+w)]}
(see Sec.
2.6
).
Hence, the choice
\alpha={\textstyle{3\over 2}}(1+w)
and
\delta={\textstyle{1\over 2}}(1+3w)
allows to
compensate for the
evolution of the scale factor.
The resulting momentum equation (for
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
) is

\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}\tilde{u}^{i})+\partial_{j}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}\tilde{u}^{i}\tilde{u}^{j})+a^{\beta-\chi+1+3w}\,\frac{\partial_{i}\tilde{p}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}(3w-1)\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,.

(81)

Then, the choice
\chi=\beta+2\delta=4+3w
leads
to the following energy and momentum equations,

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2})\,+

\displaystyle\,\partial_{i}(\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i})=0\,,

(82a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}(\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}\tilde{u}^{i})\,+

\displaystyle\,\partial_{j}(\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i}\tilde{u}^{j})+\partial_{i}\tilde{p}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}(3w-1)\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i}\,,

(82b)

where the scale-factor dependence only remains on the right-hand
side of the momentum equation, proportional to
{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\cal H}_{\alpha}
, which is
a constant due to the particular choice of
\tau_{\alpha}
.
These equations allow to use an
\alpha
-time that follows the Universe
expansion with a minimal dependence on the scale factor
to study baryon evolution in a generic background.
They generalize the super-comoving coordinates
introduced in
Banerjee and Jedamzik (2004)
for
w=0
.
Furthermore, when the background is dominated by radiation
w={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
, even though the
particles of the fluid are dust, the friction term vanishes.

Relativistic
u^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)

or
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}

Liang (1977)
;
Brandenburg et al. (1996)

)

)

1

Table 1
:
Summary of the fluid variables choice of
scaling
\tilde{\rho}=a^{\beta}\rho
,
\tilde{p}=a^{\chi}\,p
,

a^{\alpha-1}{\rm\,d}\tau_{\alpha}={\rm\,d}\tau
, and

\tilde{u}^{i}=a^{\delta}u^{i}
.
The dependence on
w
of the super-comoving variables are
a generalization proposed in this work with respect to the
w=0
case

Martel and Shapiro (1998)
.

Table
1
summarizes the different choices of the
scaling of the
fluid variables discussed.
For relativistic bulk fluid velocities, it will in general be useful
to consider
\beta=\chi=4
,
\alpha=1
(conformal time), and
\delta=0
,
corresponding to the conformal transformation discussed at the beginning of the section, as
this choice leads to conformally flat equations when
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.
On the other hand,
for subrelativistic bulk velocities, it is possible to find a
conformally flat energy equation even when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\neq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
with the
choice
\beta=\chi=3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})
and
\alpha=1+\delta
.
The resulting
momentum equation presents an
a^{2\delta}
dependence in the pressure gradient and a Hubble friction [cf. Eq. (
78b
)].
For dust, when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
,
\tilde{p}
and
\tilde{\rho}
become
decoupled and it is possible to choose
\chi=\beta+2\delta\neq\beta
to get rid of scale-factor dependence in the pressure gradient.
Two useful choices then either make the momentum equation conformally
flat or compensate the time coordinate with
the Universe expansion.
The former choice corresponds to the super-comoving
coordinates
\alpha=2
and
\delta=1
, proposed in
Martel and Shapiro (1998)
,
which
provides conformally flat fluid equations.
The latter choice generalizes
the super-comoving coordinates suggested for
w=0
in
Banerjee and Jedamzik (2004)
.
It corresponds to
\alpha={\textstyle{3\over 2}}(1+w)
and
\delta={\textstyle{1\over 2}}(1+3w)
,
resulting in a Hubble friction
\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,(3w-1)\,\tilde{\rho}\tilde{u}^{i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}
with a constant
{\cal H}_{\alpha}
in
\tau_{\alpha}
.
The two different choices might be useful in different circumstances,
depending on whether we want to use results from usual fluid dynamics
exploiting the conformal invariance of the equations, or whether
we prefer to use a time variable that evolves compensating for the
evolution of the scale factor, yielding then an additional Hubble
friction term in the usual fluid dynamic equations.

3.4
Conservation form of relativistic fluid dynamics

The equations of motion found in Sec.
3.3
, together
with an equation of state
p=c_{\rm s}^{2}\rho
, already
constitute a closed system of equations that can be solved
to compute the stress-energy tensor components
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}\tilde{T}^{0\mu}+

\displaystyle\,a^{\alpha-1}\partial_{j}\tilde{T}^{j\mu}=\tilde{f}_{H}^{\mu}\,,

(83)

corresponding to Eqs. (
73
) for
\delta=0
.
For this choice,
\tilde{T}^{ij}=a^{2+\beta}\,T^{ij}
,
and the Hubble friction due to the expansion of the Universe,

\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{H}
, is taken from Eq. (
74
),

\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}=\bigl[(\beta-4)\,\tilde{T}^{00}-\tilde{T}\bigr]{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,,\qquad\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}=\,(\beta-4)\,\tilde{T}^{0i}\,{\cal H}_{\alpha}\,.

(84)

These equations are expressed in the so-called conservation form, which is more compact than the non-conservation form
of relativistic fluid dynamics that we will discuss in Sec.
3.5

[already presented in Eqs. (
2
)].
The latter system has the advantage that it directly
solves for the primitive fluid variables
\tilde{\rho}
and
u^{i}
, while in
the conservation form the primitive variables need to be
non-linearly reconstructed from the stress-energy
components
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
.
Note that this terminology only refers to the structure of the equations
and it is related to the conservation of fluxes at the discrete cells in numerical
simulations
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
, with particular relevance
for finite volume methods
Anderson Jr. (1992)
.
However,
both forms describe the same
conservation laws of the fluid and, hence, they are equivalent in the continuum
limit.
For a particular application, it is not always obvious which form is
more suitable. Hence, it is useful to test and compare the numerical
solutions using both methodologies.

In general, a system of partial differential
equations is said to be in the conservation form if it
is expressed in the following way

\partial_{\tau_{\alpha}}{\cal U}^{a}+\partial_{j}{\cal S}^{aj}={\cal F}^{a}\,,

(85)

for an array of variables
{\cal U}^{a}
, fluxes
{\cal S}^{aj}
, and forces
{\cal F}^{a}
.
In the case of perfect-fluid relativistic fluid dynamics in an expanding
background, taking
{\cal U}^{\mu}=\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
,

{\cal S}^{\mu j}=a^{\alpha-1}\tilde{T}^{\mu j}
, and

{\cal F}^{\mu}=\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{H}
, Eq. (
83
) is recovered.

The conservation form requires to express the primitive variables
\tilde{\rho}
and
u^{i}

in terms of the variables
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
to
compute
\tilde{T}^{ij}
.
Once we have a closed form for the fluxes
\tilde{T}^{\mu j}
and forces
\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{H}

in terms of
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
, the system in Eq. (
83
) can be
numerically computed.
Assuming a generic but constant
c_{\rm s}^{2}=\tilde{p}/\tilde{\rho}
,
we first compute the following variables,

\tilde{T}^{00}=(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\tilde{\rho}\,\biggl(\gamma^{2}-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\biggr)\,,\qquad\tilde{T}^{0i}\tilde{T}^{0i}=(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}\tilde{\rho}^{2}\gamma^{2}(\gamma^{2}-1)\,,

(86)

and define the ratio

r^{2}=\frac{\tilde{T}^{0i}\tilde{T}^{0i}}{({\tilde{T}^{00}})^{2}}=\frac{\gamma^{2}(\gamma^{2}-1)}{\Bigl(\gamma^{2}-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}}\,,

(87)

which is shown in Fig.
1
as a function of
\gamma
for different
values of
c_{\rm s}^{2}
.
We note that for this relation to be valid we require
\delta=0
, as any rescaling
of
u^{i}
would affect the relation between
\gamma^{2}
and
u^{2}
.
The previous equation can be solved for
\gamma^{2}
,

\gamma^{2}=\frac{1}{2(1-r^{2})}\left[1-2r^{2}\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}+\sqrt{1-4r^{2}\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}}}\ \right]\,,

(88)

where we take the positive root of the quadratic equation to ensure

\gamma^{2}\geq 1
when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\leq 1
.
Then, the fluid primitive variables can be expressed in terms of
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}

and
\gamma^{2}
as

\tilde{\rho}=\frac{\tilde{T}^{00}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\gamma^{2}-c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,,\qquad u^{i}=\frac{\tilde{T}^{0i}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}=\frac{\tilde{T}^{0i}}{\tilde{T}^{00}}\biggl[1-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\gamma^{2}}\biggr]\,,

(89)

such that the flux
\tilde{T}^{ij}
is

\tilde{T}^{ij}=\bigl[(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\gamma^{2}u^{i}u^{j}+c_{\rm s}^{2}\delta^{ij}\bigr]\,\tilde{\rho}=\frac{\tilde{T}^{0i}\tilde{T}^{0j}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}+c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\tilde{\rho}\,\delta^{ij}\,.

(90)

Using this formalism,
the non-linear relativistic system of equations can be solved
evolving the
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
components
and then the primitive fluid variables
\tilde{\rho}

and
u^{i}
can be reconstructed from
T^{0\mu}
.
A similar procedure has been used, e.g., in the Higgsless formulation,
to numerically compute the fluid perturbations induced in the
primordial plasma by first-order phase transitions in
Caprini et al. (2025)
;
Jinno et al. (2021)
;
Jinno et al. (2023)
.

Figure 1
:
Ratio
r=\sqrt{\tilde{T}^{0i}\tilde{T}^{0i}}/\tilde{T}^{00}
of a perfect fluid as a function of the Lorentz factor

\gamma
for different constant values of
c_{\rm s}^{2}=\tilde{p}/\tilde{\rho}
.

Conservation form in the subrelativistic limit

In the subrelativistic limit,
u^{2}\ll 1
,
the components of the
stress-energy tensor become

\tilde{T}^{00}=\tilde{\rho}\,,\qquad\tilde{T}^{0i}=(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\tilde{\rho}u^{i}\,,\qquad\tilde{T}^{ij}=\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\tilde{T}^{0i}\tilde{T}^{0j}}{\tilde{T}^{00}}+c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\tilde{T}^{00}\,\delta^{ij}\,.

(91)

These relations are tempting due to their simplicity,
as they already provide a straightforward relation between

\tilde{T}^{ij}
and
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
.
However, as we have already mentioned,
time derivatives of
\gamma^{2}
lead to additional
terms in the energy conservation equation that are not negligible
in the subrelativistic regime, as we will prove in the following.
Therefore, if one sets
\gamma^{2}\to 1
to express
\tilde{T}^{ij}
in terms
of
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
, indirectly
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}\to 0
is being assumed
and, hence, subrelativistic terms are ignored.
To show this and recover all relevant terms, let us expand
Eq. (
88
) up to first order in
r^{2}\equiv\tilde{T}^{0i}\,\tilde{T}^{0i}/(\tilde{T}^{00})^{2}\sim{\cal O}(u^{2})
,

\gamma^{2}=1+\frac{r^{2}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}}+{\cal O}(u^{4})\,.

(92)

The
r^{2}\to 0
limit is equivalent to the subrelativistic limit, as can
be seen in Fig.
1
.
In fact, the expansion up to order
r^{2n}
is equivalent
to an expansion up to order
u^{2n}
.
Then, substituting
\gamma^{2}
up to first order in
r^{2}
into

\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
, we find

\tilde{T}^{00}=\biggl(1+\frac{r^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\biggr)\tilde{\rho}+{\cal O}(u^{4})\,,\qquad\tilde{T}^{0i}=\biggl(1+c_{\rm s}^{2}+\frac{r^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\biggr)\tilde{\rho}\,u^{i}+{\cal O}(u^{4})\,,

(93)

which allows us to express
\tilde{T}^{ij}
up to first order in
u^{2}
,

\tilde{T}^{ij}=\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\tilde{T}^{0i}\tilde{T}^{0j}}{\tilde{T}^{00}}\biggl(1+\frac{r^{2}c_{\rm s}^{2}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}}\biggr)+c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{T}^{00}\biggl(1-\frac{r^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\biggr)\,\delta^{ij}+{\cal O}(u^{4})\,.

(94)

This relation then
takes into account the corresponding corrections in
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
up to first order in
u^{2}

and leads to the correct subrelativistic limit, as we show in the following.
Therefore, this is an appropriate way to close the system of
subrelativistic equations in their conservation form.

To explicitly show the subrelativistic corrections due to the inclusion
of
r^{2}
in the time derivatives, let us take the conformal time derivatives of Eq. (
93
)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{00}=\,

\displaystyle\,\partial_{\tau}\tilde{\rho}+\frac{\tilde{\rho}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\partial_{\tau}\bigl[r^{2}+{\cal O}(r^{4})\bigr]\,,

(95a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{0i}=\,

\displaystyle\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\partial_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}u^{i})+\frac{\tilde{\rho}u^{i}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\partial_{\tau}\bigl[r^{2}+{\cal O}(r^{4})\bigr]\,.

(95b)

Note that the next-to-leading-order term is proportional to

\partial_{\tau}r^{2}
, which can be expressed as

\partial_{\tau}r^{2}=(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}\,\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}=(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}\,\gamma^{2}\,\partial_{\tau}u^{2}=(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}\,\partial_{\tau}u^{2}+{\cal O}(u^{4})\,.

(96)

Therefore, this term is proportional to
\partial_{\tau}u^{2}
.
To estimate
\partial_{\tau}u^{2}
, let us first expand the momentum equation,
setting
\mu=i
in Eq. (
83
),
as we will do for the full
relativistic system in Sec.
3.5
, and using Eq. (
95b
) to find

u^{i}\,\partial_{\tau}\tilde{\rho}+\tilde{\rho}\,\partial_{\tau}u^{i}+\frac{\tilde{\rho}u^{i}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})^{2}}\partial_{\tau}r^{2}+\,\partial_{j}\bigl(\tilde{\rho}u^{i}u^{j}\bigr)+\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\partial_{i}\tilde{\rho}=\frac{\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}+{\cal O}(u^{2})\,.

(97)

Then,
taking the product of
u^{i}
with the momentum equation,
and keeping only leading-order terms, we find

{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\tilde{\rho}\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\partial_{\tau}u^{2}=-c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{\rho}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}+{\cal O}(u^{2})\,,

(98)

where the leading-order terms in the
right-hand side correspond to the work done by the
pressure gradient,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}
,
and the Hubble friction,
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}
.
As expected for perfect fluids,
the work done by these forces is a reversible process and,
hence, it does not produce entropy
Kundu and Cohen (2008)
.
Plugging Eq. (
98
) back into Eqs. (
95
),
the correct subrelativistic limit of

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{0\mu}
can be found,
taking into account the contribution from
\partial_{\tau}u^{2}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{00}=\,

\displaystyle\,\partial_{\tau}\tilde{\rho}-2\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{\rho}+2\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}\,,

(99a)

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{0i}=\,

\displaystyle\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\partial_{\tau}(\tilde{\rho}u^{i})-2\,u^{i}\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{\rho}+2\,u^{i}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}\,.

(99b)

Equations (
98
) and (
99
) show that neglecting the next-to-leading
order term
r^{2}
in the time derivatives of the stress-energy tensor components in
Eq. (
93
) is only justified in the subrelativistic limit when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
but not
for a generic
c_{\rm s}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)
.
Substituting this term back in the energy and momentum
conservation equations,
we find

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,=

\displaystyle\,-(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1-c_{\rm s}^{2})}\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}(\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}-2\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H})\,,

(100a)

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\biggl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}}\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\biggr]-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggl(\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}-\frac{2c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}\biggr)

\displaystyle\,-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}}\,,

(100b)

with the corrections found indicated in red.
This set of equations is equivalent to the one presented
in the introduction; cf. Eqs. (
7
), in the absence of external forces
(only the Hubble friction has been included so far).
They generalize the set of equations used in previous work to
values of the speed of sound different than
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
but still relativistic,
i.e.,
c_{\rm s}^{2}\sim{\cal O}(1)
,
introducing terms that break conformal invariance, i.e., Hubble friction

\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{H}
.
For all values
c_{\rm s}^{2}\neq 0
,
these expressions include
corrections to the energy
and momentum equations with respect to previous work in the
subrelativistic limit (cf. Eqs. (
50
) for a radiation-dominated fluid).

Using an appropriate subrelativistic limit of the stress-energy
tensor components, the
subrelativistic Euler equations in their non-conservation form,
explicitly describing the
dynamics of the comoving energy density
\tilde{\rho}

and the velocity field
\textstyle u
,
have been found.
In the next section, we compute the relativistic version of the
conservation laws in the non-conservation form.

3.5
Non-conservation form of relativistic fluid dynamics

The non-conservation form of the fluid equations
is obtained expressing explicitly
the stress-energy tensor components in terms of the primitive
fluid variables,
\tilde{\rho}
and
\textstyle u
.
In the following, the extension of the system of
equations given in Eqs. (
100
)
to the fully relativistic regime is done,
up to our knowledge,
for the first time for a perfect fluid in an expanding background.
For compactness, we set
\alpha=1
and
\delta=0
in the following, but
the equations can be generalized to any rescaling choice.

3.5.1 Relativistic energy equation

Let us start with the equation of energy conservation taking
\mu=0

in Eq. (
83
), and dividing the equation
by
(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}
,

D_{\tau}\ln(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})+D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=\frac{\partial_{\tau}\tilde{p}+\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}}{(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}}\,,

(101)

where
D_{\tau}=\partial_{\tau}+u_{i}\partial^{i}
is the material
derivative.
In terms of a constant
speed of sound
\tilde{p}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{\rho}
, the energy equation becomes

D_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}+D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\gamma^{2}}\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}\,.

(102)

As discussed,
previous work considered this expression directly taking the
subrelativistic limit
u^{2}\ll 1
for
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}

and
\beta=4
,
such that
\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}=0
.
However, as shown in the previous section,
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}=\gamma^{4}\partial_{\tau}u^{2}=\gamma^{2}\partial_{\tau}r^{2}/(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})
cannot be
neglected in the subrelativistic regime.
In the following, we will keep all terms to find the fully relativistic
conservation laws of perfect fluids.

An alternative (but, of course, equivalent) version of the energy conservation
equation in Eq. (
102
) can be found projecting the conservation laws in Eq. (
63
) with the
comoving four-velocity
\tilde{U}^{\mu}=aU^{\mu}=\gamma(1,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{})
,

\tilde{U}_{\nu}\bigl(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}-\tilde{f}_{H}^{\nu}\bigr)=\tilde{U}_{\nu}(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\tilde{U}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{U}^{\nu}+\tilde{U}_{\nu}\tilde{U}^{\nu}\partial_{\mu}[(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\tilde{U}^{\mu}]+\tilde{U}^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{p}-\tilde{f}^{H}_{\mu})\,.

(103)

Taking into account the normalization condition of
\tilde{U}^{\mu}
, i.e.,
\tilde{U}^{\mu}\tilde{U}_{\mu}=-1
,
it follows that
\tilde{U}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{U}^{\nu}=0
.
Note that here
we use the Minkowski metric to lower/rise indices for comoving tensors, such that

\tilde{U}_{\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}\tilde{U}^{\mu}
, since we can study the equations
in Minkowski space-time after a conformal transformation.
Hence,

\displaystyle\tilde{U}_{\nu}\bigl(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}-\tilde{f}_{H}^{\nu}\bigr)=

\displaystyle\,-\partial_{\mu}[(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\tilde{U}^{\mu}]+\tilde{U}^{\mu}(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{p}-\tilde{f}_{\mu}^{H})=0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\ \tilde{U}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{\rho}+(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\partial_{\mu}\tilde{U}^{\mu}+\tilde{U}^{\mu}\tilde{f}_{\mu}^{H}=0\,.

(104)

As a side note, notice that promoting derivatives
into covariant derivatives, the following continuity equation
is valid for any metric tensor
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
,

-U_{\nu}T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=U^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\rho+(p+\rho)\,\theta=0\,,

(105)

where we have defined
the relativistic fluid expansion scalar
\theta=U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}
.

Going back to the FLRW metric tensor,
taking
\tilde{p}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{\rho}
with constant
c_{\rm s}^{2}
,
one finds an alternative version of the energy conservation
equation

D_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}+(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})D_{\tau}\ln\gamma+(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}(\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H})\,.

(106)

In order to get rid of the
D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}=2D_{\tau}\ln\gamma
term,
we can directly combine Eqs. (
102
) and (
106
), since both equations need to be
satisfied.
Actually, we can observe that taking
D_{\tau}\ln\gamma\to 0
in the
subrelativistic limit leads to a different result in Eqs. (
102
) and (
106
),
already proving that this assumption cannot be correct.
Some cumbersome but direct algebra brings us the relativistic conservation equation,

\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}={\cal F}_{H}^{0}\,,

(107)

where the Hubble friction in the energy equation is

{\cal F}_{H}^{0}=\frac{1}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}\bigl[\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}(1+u^{2})-2\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}\bigr]=\Bigl[(\beta-4)+\frac{1+u^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}(1-3c_{\rm s}^{2})\Bigr]\,{\cal H}\,.

(108)

This Hubble term depends on
\beta
and only for
\beta=4
(which yields
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}=0
)
and
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
it
vanishes in the fully
relativistic case.
However, we
keep
\beta
in this term to allow for a general choice of the scaling of
\tilde{T}^{0}_{\ \,0}
using
Eq. (
74
).
Note that the first equality in this expression can be used to add any external
forces to the relativistic energy equation, e.g., imperfect
and electromagnetic forces; see Secs.
4
and
6.2
,
respectively.

Taking the
u^{2}\ll 1
limit in Eq. (
107
)
shows the correction in the subrelativistic energy equation
[cf. Eq. (
50a
)] that has been
omitted in previous work,

\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}=-(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}(1-c_{\rm s}^{2})}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}+\bigl[\beta-3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\bigr]\,{\cal H}\,.

(109)

Furthermore, as discussed in Sec.
3.3
, contrary to the fully relativistic case,
it is now possible to choose
\beta=3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})
to get rid of

\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}
.

3.5.2 Relativistic momentum equation

Let us now proceed to compute the momentum equation, given by the
spatial components of Eq. (
83
).
After dividing the equation with
(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}
,
one finds

D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}D_{\tau}\ln\bigl[(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}\bigr]+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{})=-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}}\,.

(110)

For an equation of state with a constant
speed of sound,
\tilde{p}=c_{\rm s}^{2}\tilde{\rho}
,

D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\bigl(D_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,+D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\bigr)=-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}\,.

(111)

Using the energy conservation equation
(
102
),
Euler equation becomes

D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\gamma^{2}}\biggl(c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggr)=-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}\,.

(112)

Again, we note that previous work has considered the
subrelativistic limit of this equation setting
\gamma^{2}=1
,
and used
\beta=4
,
such that
\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}=0
.
Similarly as with the energy equation, the subrelativistic term
contained in
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}
that modifies the energy
equation also enters the momentum equation.

The relativistic Euler equation is then obtained using the energy equation to substitute

\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}
[cf. Eq. (
107
)] into Eq. (
112
),

\displaystyle D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,c_{\rm s}^{2}}{(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})\gamma^{2}}\biggl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\biggr]-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}}{\gamma^{2}}+\boldsymbol{\cal F}_{H}\,,

(113)

where the Hubble friction in the momentum equation is

\boldsymbol{\cal F}_{H}=-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}\biggl(\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}-\frac{2c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}\biggr)+\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}=\frac{3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}}{\gamma^{2}}\,.

(114)

We note that the Hubble friction in the momentum equation becomes
independent of the value of
\beta
and vanishes when
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.
The subrelativistic limit of the momentum equation is found taking
u^{2}\ll 1
in Eqs. (
113
) and (
114
),

\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\biggl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+{\color[rgb]{1,0,0}\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\biggr]-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}+(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}\,,

(115)

where the correction with respect to previous work is indicated in red.

The system of Eqs. (
107
) and (
113
) is found to be remarkably simplified after the
procedure described in this section, as one finds that their modification
with respect to their subrelativistic counterpart is restricted to the
prefactors
(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})^{-1}
and
(1-u^{2})(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})^{-1}
in the
energy and momentum equations, respectively, as well as an additional
\gamma^{-2}=1-u^{2}

in the forces of the momentum equation (e.g., pressure
gradient and Hubble friction).
Taking the subrelativistic limit of the energy and momentum equations, they
are found to be
those given in Eqs. (
100
),
computed in the previous section directly from taking the
subrelativistic limit of the conservation form of the fluid
equations.
This again confirms the necessity to keep terms of order
r^{2}
in the conservation
formalism to recover the correct subrelativistic limit.

Alternatively, we can find an equivalent momentum equation contracting the conservation
laws with the projection tensor
\tilde{h}_{\nu i}=\tilde{U}_{\nu}\tilde{U}_{i}+\eta_{\nu i}
.
In the first place, a direct evaluation shows

\tilde{h}_{\nu}^{\ \,i}\bigl(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}-\tilde{f}_{H}^{\nu}\bigr)=\tilde{U}^{i}\tilde{U}_{\nu}\bigl(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}-\tilde{f}_{H}^{\nu}\bigr)+\bigl(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu i}-\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}\bigr)=0\,.

(116)

This is equivalent to subtracting the energy equation [cf. Eq. (
106
)]
multiplied by
\textstyle u
[note the negative sign in Eq. (
104
)]
and add the momentum equation [cf. Eq. (
111
)] multiplied by
(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\gamma
.
This procedure provides a simplified momentum equation.
To directly show this, we can use the properties of the projection tensor,
\tilde{U}^{\mu}\tilde{h}_{{\mu\nu}}=0

and
\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}\tilde{h}_{\nu\lambda}=\tilde{h}^{\mu}_{\ \lambda}
, and
express
\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}=\tilde{\rho}\tilde{U}^{\mu}\tilde{U}^{\nu}+\tilde{p}\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}
.
Hence, applying these properties and taking into account that
\tilde{U}_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{U}^{\mu}=0
,
we find

\tilde{h}_{\nu i}(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}-\tilde{f}^{\nu}_{H})=(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\tilde{U}^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{U}_{i}+\tilde{h}^{\mu}_{\ \,i}\partial_{\mu}\tilde{p}-\tilde{U}^{\mu}\tilde{U}_{i}\tilde{f}^{H}_{\mu}-\tilde{f}^{H}_{i}=0\,.

(117)

As done above, this equation can be generalized for any metric tensor
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)

(p+\rho)a^{\mu}+\nabla^{\mu}p=0\,,

(118)

where
a^{\mu}=U^{\nu}U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\nu}}
is the acceleration four-vector and
\nabla^{\mu}=h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}
.

3.5.3 Evolution of the Lorentz factor

We proceed to explicitly compute an evolution equation for
u^{2}
or, equivalently,
for the Lorentz factor, as this equation is in general enlightening for studying the kinetic energy,

\tilde{\rho}_{\rm kin}=(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\gamma^{2}u^{2}
,
and to explicitly show its correction in the subrelativistic limit.
The relativistic evolution equation of the Lorentz
factor can be computed from the momentum equation [cf. Eq. (
113
)],
by contracting it with the velocity field
\textstyle u
,
as done in the subrelativistic limit in Eq. (
98
),

D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}=2\gamma^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot(D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{})=\frac{2c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\biggl[u^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-\frac{1}{(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\gamma^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\biggr]+2\gamma^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\boldsymbol{\cal F}_{H}\,,

(119)

where the term due to the Hubble friction is

2\gamma^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\boldsymbol{\cal F}_{H}=-\frac{2u^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}}{\tilde{\rho}}+\frac{2}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{\tilde{\rho}}=2\,\frac{3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\,u^{2}{\cal H}\,.

(120)

Taking the subrelativistic limit of Eq. (
119
) we find

\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}=-\frac{2c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{2}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{H}}{\tilde{\rho}}\,,

(121)

explicitly showing that this term cannot be neglected in the subrelativistic limit.

As already discussed, comparing
Eqs. (
100
)
to the equations used in previous work for a radiation-dominated
fluid with
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
and
\beta=4
, such that
\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{H}=0

[cf. Eqs. (
49
)], we find an additional
{\textstyle{1\over 2}}
factor multiplying one of the terms in both the energy and the momentum
equations, as indicated explicitly in red in Eqs. (
50
).
This is due to the fact that even if
\gamma^{2}\to 1
,
D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}
contains subrelativistic
corrections, as indicated in Eq. (
121
).
Only when
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
(i.e., matter-dominated fluid) and
\beta=4
(
\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}=0
), this term can be
neglected in the subrelativistic limit and taking
\gamma^{2}\to 1

from the beginning is justified.
This term leads to the following correction in Eqs. (
49
) with respect to the equations found
when
\gamma^{2}\to 1
and
\partial_{\tau}\gamma^{2}\to 0
in previous work,

(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\to\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\,,

(122)

in both the energy and momentum equations.

3.6
Conservation laws for specific values of
c_{\rm s}^{2}

Let us now consider two special cases: fluids dominated by radiation
(
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
) and matter (
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
).

3.6.1 Equations for radiation-dominated fluids

For
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
, the relativistic energy and
momentum equations are

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}=

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{3-u^{2}}\bigl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\bigl(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\bigr)\ln\tilde{\rho}\bigr]+(\beta-4)\,{\cal H}\,,

(123a)

\displaystyle D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{1-u^{2}}{3-u^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\bigl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+{\textstyle{1\over 2}}\bigl(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\bigr)\ln\tilde{\rho}\bigr]-\frac{1-u^{2}}{4}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,,

(123b)

where the momentum equation is conformally flat for any choice of
\beta
, while
the Hubble term in the energy equation vanishes when
\beta=4
.
These conservation laws reduce to those in Eqs. (
50
)
in the subrelativistic limit.
In the ultrarelativistic limit
u^{2}\to 1
, the equations become

\lim_{u^{2}\to 1}D_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}=-2\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,,\qquad\lim_{u^{2}\to 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=0\,.

(124)

3.6.2 Equations for matter-dominated fluids

For
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
, the fluid equations become those for usual fluid dynamics with an additional Hubble friction term that takes into account the Universe
expansion,

D_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=(\beta-3+u^{2})\,{\cal H}\,,\qquad D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=-(1-u^{2})\biggl(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}+\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggr)\,,

(125)

where the Hubble friction that appears in
the momentum equation is independent of the choice of

\beta
.
In the subrelativistic limit,
the equations reduce to

\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+(\beta-3)\,{\cal H}\,,\qquad\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}}{\tilde{\rho}}\,.

(126)

Then, for subrelativistic flows with
c_{\rm s}^{2}\ll 1
, it is clear that it is more
convenient to choose
\beta=3
in the scaling, as it allows to get rid of the
Hubble term in the energy equation.
As discussed in Sec.
3.3
, the choice of super-comoving
variables
\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{}=a\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}

(
\delta=1
),
\alpha=2
, and
\tilde{p}=a^{5}p
(
\chi=5
), together
with
\beta=3
, allows us to find conformally-flat equations,

\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau_{\alpha}}\ln\tilde{\rho}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,,\qquad\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau_{\alpha}}\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{}=-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{p}}{\tilde{\rho}}\,.

(127)

3.7
Sound waves

In this section, linear perturbations of the perfect
fluid equations of motion are studied.
For this purpose, we consider that the background energy density
is homogeneous and
evolves with the scale factor as
\bar{\rho}\sim a^{-3\,(1+w)}

(see Sec.
2.6
) for a background equation of state
\bar{p}=w\bar{\rho}
.
Let us consider perturbations
\rho_{1}
and
\textstyle u
over the background
at rest.
The energy and momentum equations up to first order in perturbations are [cf. Eqs. (
100
)],

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\tilde{\rho}_{1}=

\displaystyle-(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\bigl[\beta-3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\bigr]\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}\,{\cal H}\,,

(128a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{\tilde{\rho}_{0}}+(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}\,,

(128b)

where
\tilde{\rho}_{0}=a^{3\,(1+w)}\,\bar{\rho}
corresponds
to the comoving energy density of the background, constant in time.
In first place, we note that the correction described in previous
sections, which applies to the term
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}
,
does not affect the linearized equations but it becomes relevant
in the non-linear regime.
Secondly, it is convenient to choose

\beta=3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})
to get rid of the Hubble
friction in the energy equation,
such that
\tilde{\rho}_{1}=a^{\beta}\rho_{1}
.
We note that
c_{\rm s}^{2}
and
w
are not, in general, required to be equal,
allowing the total energy of the Universe to contain
additional contributions besides the one from the fluid.
In terms of the normalized energy density perturbations
\lambda
, defined such
that

\lambda=\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\tilde{\rho}_{1}}{\tilde{\rho}_{0}}=\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\rho_{1}}{\bar{\rho}}\,a^{3\,(c_{\rm s}^{2}-w)}\,,

(129)

where
(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\tilde{\rho}_{0}
is the comoving background
enthalpy, the energy and momentum equations are

\partial_{\tau}\lambda=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}-c_{\rm s}^{2}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\lambda\,.

(130)

We note that for any other choice of
\beta
, the energy
equation would present an additional source term

\partial_{\tau}\lambda=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\frac{\beta-3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,{\cal H}\,.

(131)

One directly finds from the momentum equation
that fluid perturbations are aligned with the
gradients of the energy density perturbations (corresponding to sound waves).
Equivalently, in Fourier space,

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=u_{\parallel}\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle k$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle k$}}}{}
.
On the other hand,
perpendicular fluid perturbations follow an exponential
decay (for
c_{\rm s}^{2}<{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
) or growth (for a stiff equation of state
c_{\rm s}^{2}>{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
) due to the Hubble friction,

u_{\perp}=u_{\perp}(\tau_{0})\exp\Bigl[(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}{\cal H}(\tau^{\prime}){\rm\,d}\tau^{\prime}\Bigr]\,.

(132)

Combining both energy and momentum
equations, we find damped wave equations for
\lambda

and
u_{\parallel}
(expressed in momentum space
\textstyle k
),

\displaystyle\partial^{2}_{\tau}\lambda\,-
