\displaystyle\,(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,{\cal H}\,\partial_{\tau}\lambda+c_{\rm s}^{2}k^{2}\lambda=0\,,

(133a)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}^{2}u_{\parallel}\,-

\displaystyle\,(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,{\cal H}\,\partial_{\tau}u_{\parallel}+\bigl[c_{\rm s}^{2}k^{2}-{\cal H}^{\prime}(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\bigr]u_{\parallel}=0\,.

(133b)

When
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
, the system reduces to free-propagating sound
waves with no Hubble friction, whose dispersion relation is
\omega^{2}=c_{\rm s}^{2}k^{2}
.
When
c_{\rm s}^{2}\neq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
,
the sound-wave perturbations present a
time-dependent Hubble friction
(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,{\cal H}\,\partial_{\tau}{\cal U}^{a}
with

{\cal U}^{a}=\{\lambda,u_{\parallel}\}
,
and the parallel velocity field has an additional modification to the
angular frequency
\omega^{2}\equiv c_{\rm s}^{2}k^{2}-{\cal H}^{\prime}\,(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)
.
In general, for a background equation of state
\bar{p}=w\bar{\rho}
, when

w>-{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
we can express the Hubble rate and its
derivative in the following way

{\cal H}=\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}=\frac{2}{1+3w}\frac{1}{\tau}\,,\qquad{\cal H}^{\prime}=-\frac{2}{1+3w}\frac{1}{\tau^{2}}\,.

(134)

Therefore, the homogeneous wave equations
become

\displaystyle\lambda^{\prime\prime}\,-2\sigma\lambda^{\prime}/\tau+\omega^{2}\lambda=0\,,\qquad u_{\parallel}^{\prime\prime}\,-2\sigma u_{\parallel}^{\prime}/\tau+(\omega^{2}+2\sigma/\tau^{2})u_{\parallel}=0\,,

(135a)

where
\sigma=(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)/(1+3w)
.
Their analytical solution is

Figure 2
:
Evolution of the sound-wave energy density
perturbations
\lambda
for an arbitrary choice
of the initial conditions,
c_{2}=0
.
Free-propagating waves are found when
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.

\displaystyle\lambda(\omega\tau)=

\displaystyle\,c_{1}\,(\omega\tau)^{\sigma+1}j_{\sigma}(\omega\tau)+c_{2}\,(\omega\tau)^{\sigma+1}y_{\sigma}(\omega\tau)\,,

(136a)

\displaystyle u_{\parallel}(\omega\tau)=

\displaystyle\,c_{3}\,(\omega\tau)^{\sigma+1}\,j_{\sigma-1}(\omega\tau)+c_{4}\,(\omega\tau)^{\sigma+1}\,y_{\sigma-1}(\omega\tau)\,,

(136b)

where

j_{a}
and
y_{a}
are the spherical
Bessel functions of the first and
the second kind, respectively.
These solutions have been recently considered in
Giombi et al. (2025b)
;
Giombi et al. (2025a)
to study the production of gravitational
waves from sound waves in an expanding background dominated
by the fluid with

w=c_{\rm s}^{2}\neq{\textstyle{1\over 3}}
.
Figure
2
shows the evolution of
\lambda
for arbitrary initial conditions such that
c_{2}=0

for different values of
c_{\rm s}^{2}
and
w
.

4
Relativistic imperfect fluid dynamics

The zero-th order approximation in fluid dynamics has been
used in Sec.
3
to characterize perfect fluids.
This approximation is valid under the
assumption that the distribution function
f
of the
fluid particles is the one found in local thermal equilibrium (LTE),
i.e., the Maxwell-Boltzmann distribution in the
subrelativistic limit.
In the relativistic regime, one finds that the LTE
distribution can be Maxwell-Jüttner, Bose-Einstein,
or Fermi-Dirac; see
Cercignani and Kremer (2002)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)

for details.

The perfect fluid description holds when the collisions in the
system are frequent, such that they drive the fluid parcels
to LTE
in a time scale
l_{\rm mfp}/v
that is shorter than the time scales
characterizing the fluid, being
v
the typical particle velocity and
l_{\rm mfp}
the mean free path.
This corresponds to the limit of small Knudsen
number, Kn
=l_{\rm mfp}/L\ll 1
, i.e., small mean free
path of the fluid particles compared to a characteristic length
scale
L
of the fluid fields.

In Sec.
4.1
,
the distribution function is expanded using the Knudsen number
as the perturbative parameter, following Chapman-Enskog theory

Chapman and Cowling (1970)
.
This allows to describe subrelativistic imperfect fluids in the first-order
fluid dynamics approximation, leading to Navier-Stokes
shear stress tensor
describing viscosity, and to heat fluxes, described
by Fourier’s law of conductivity.
In Sec.
4.2
, we review the estimates of the viscous shear and bulk coefficients
and of the thermal conductivity in the early Universe.
In Sec.
4.3
, Navier-Stokes viscosity
and Fourier’s heat fluxes
are presented in a covariant relativistic formulation,
following the classical
irreversible thermodynamics (CIT) approach

Eckart (1940)
;
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.
A known problem of the CIT approach
is that it leads to
fluid perturbations that are allowed to propagate at unbounded
speeds, violating
the postulates of special relativity.
This is a consequence of the parabolic nature
of the diffusion operators that
describe Navier-Stokes
viscosity in the momentum equation,
\nu\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}
,
and Fourier’s heat flux in the energy equation,

\kappa\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}^{2}T
,
being
\nu
and
\kappa
the coefficients of shear viscosity
and heat-flux conductivity, respectively.
As we show in Sec.
4.1
, these terms appear due to
the inclusion of
the following stress-energy fluxes from
deviations with respect to a pefect fluid in LTE:

\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{ij}\supset-\tilde{\nu}\,\partial_{i}\partial^{i}u^{j}

and
\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{0i}\supset\partial_{i}\tilde{q}^{i}=-\tilde{\kappa}\,\partial_{i}\partial^{i}\tilde{T}
,
respectively, with the comoving
variables
\tilde{\nu}
,
\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}}{}
,
\tilde{\kappa}
, and
\tilde{T}
defined in Sec.
4.1
.

A possible solution is to introduce relaxation times
\tau_{r}

to the equations describing the deviations with respect to LTE, motivated by the results found in kinetic theory,
as proposed in
Cattaneo (1948)
for the heat flux (expressed in Minkowski space-time),

\tau_{r}\,\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}{}=-\kappa\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}T\,,

(137)

where the inclusion of the
\tau_{r}\,\partial_{t}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}{}
term
makes the equation hyperbolic and, hence, it represents a causal
description, introducing a response time
to changes in the temperature.
Following the pioneer work of
Israel (1976)
;
Israel and Stewart (1976)
, Israel-Stewart theory
is described by dynamical equations for the transport coefficients (e.g., shear and bulk viscosity, and thermal conductivity), introduced together with
relaxation times, also known as the Maxwell-Cattaneo form of the relativistic viscous fluid dynamic equations
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
;
Romatschke (2010)
.
For example, the deviatoric viscous tensor that is subtracted to
the stress-energy tensor of a perfect fluid,
T^{ij}\supset T^{ij}_{\rm pf}-\Pi^{ij}_{2}
, is described using the
following dynamical equation (expressed in Minkowski space-time)

\tau_{\Pi}\,\partial_{t}\Pi^{ij}_{2}+\Pi^{ij}_{2}=\Pi^{ij}_{1}\,,

(138)

where
\Pi^{ij}_{1}
corresponds to the deviatoric viscous
tensor found under the CIT approach (see Secs.
4.1
and
4.3
).
These models are known as extended irreversible thermodynamics,
or second-order fluid dynamics.
They are an active topic
of research (see the review
Romatschke (2010)
) and go beyond
the scope of this work.

For simplicity and to allow to express the resulting MHD equations (see Sec.
7
) in a form that is suitable to directly apply time integrators
like Runge-Kutta methods, i.e., writing one equation for the time
derivative of each variable,
we will restrict ourselves to the CIT
approach,
leading to the Navier-Stokes
description of the viscous shear and bulk stresses
and the Fourier’s heat flux, in the subrelativistic limit.
For simulations of MHD in the early Universe,
from the onset of radiation
domination down to neutrino decoupling, as we show in Sec.
4.2
, the actual values of the
transport coefficients are very small when the primordial
plasma is near equilibrium, since they are proportional to the mean free path of the plasma
particles
Weinberg (1971)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Baym and Heiselberg (1997)
;
Ahonen (1999)
;
Arnold et al. (2000)
;
Arnold et al. (2003)
;
Arnold et al. (2006)
;
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
;
Uchida et al. (2025)
, affecting the dynamics
at scales several orders of magnitude smaller than the Hubble horizon
Caprini et al. (2009)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2020a)
.
Therefore, realistically viable simulations usually take viscous coefficients that are small enough
to not affect the large-scale dynamics, but still orders of magnitude larger than their actual
values (cf.
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Christensson et al. (2001)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2019)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2017a)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2017b)
;
Schober et al. (2020a)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2020b)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2022b)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2022a)
).
In these situations, as the simulations are not resolving the
small scales, the relativistic description is not
expected to affect the macroscopic dynamics, and this approach is justified.

For further details on relativistic fluid dynamics of imperfect
fluids we recommend the reader
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)

and references therein.

4.1
First-order imperfect fluids

Viscous effects and heat fluxes
arise due to collisions of the fluid particles that
perturb the distribution function with respect to LTE,
such that
{\rm\,d}f/{\rm\,d}t\neq 0
.
The resulting distribution function can be expanded in terms of the
Knudsen number

f(X^{\mu},p^{\mu})=f_{0}(X^{\mu},p^{\mu})+\delta f(X^{\mu},p^{\mu})+{\cal O}({\rm Kn}^{2})\,,

(139)

where
p^{\mu}
is the four-momentum,
f_{0}
is the LTE distribution (e.g., for fluids made of classical particles, Maxwell-Boltzmann, or
Maxwell-Jüttner in the relativistic limit
Jüttner (1911)
), and
\delta f
is the
first-order perturbation in the distribution function.
This approach follows Chapman-Enskog theory
Chapman and Cowling (1970)
,
and provides a fluid dynamics description
for imperfect fluids
using the Knudsen number as a perturbative parameter.
In particular, the Knudsen numbers
defined using the gradients of the fluid velocity
and the temperature to estimate the characteristic fluid
length scale
L

yield Navier-Stokes viscosity and Fourier’s law of conductivity,
respectively.

In the subrelativistic limit, the additional contribution to the stress tensor
due to first-order imperfect fluids is described by the deviatoric viscous stress tensor
\Pi^{ij}
,
such that

T^{ij}=T^{ij}_{\rm pf}-\Pi^{ij}_{\rm visc}

Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Arnold et al. (2000)
;
Kundu and Cohen (2008)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
;
Durrer and Caprini (2003)
,

\frac{\Pi^{ij}_{\rm visc}}{p+\rho}=2\,\nu\,\sigma^{ij}+\xi\,\theta\,g^{ij}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }\sigma^{ij}=S^{ij}-{\textstyle{1\over 3}}\theta\,g^{ij}\,,

(140)

where
\nu=\eta_{\rm visc}/(p+\rho)
and
\xi=\zeta_{\rm visc}/(p+\rho)
are the
kinematic shear and bulk viscosities (
\eta_{\rm visc}
and
\zeta_{\rm visc}
being the dynamic
viscous coefficients).
The traceless rate-of-strain tensor is
\sigma^{ij}

with

S^{ij}=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(U^{i;j}+U^{j;i}\bigr)={\textstyle{1\over 2}}a^{-3}(\partial^{i}u^{j}+\partial^{j}u^{i}+2\,{\cal H}\,\delta^{ij})\,,

(141)

and
\theta=S^{i}_{\ i}=a^{-1}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+3{\cal H})

is the fluid expansion scalar
(already defined in Sec.
3.5
).
The comoving rate-of-strain tensor and expansion scalar are

\tilde{S}^{ij}=a^{3}S^{ij}

and
\tilde{\theta}=a\theta
.
A kinematic approach to describe the anisotropic
stress tensor,
T_{ij}
, as the most general rank-2 tensor to describe linear
constitutive relations (i.e., Newtonian fluids) also leads to Eq. (
140
) and corresponds
to the original derivation of the Navier-Stokes equations
Navier (1821)
;
Stokes (1880)
.
We note that the comoving deviatoric viscous
stress tensor is

\tilde{\Pi}^{ij}_{\rm visc}=a^{6}\Pi^{ij}_{\rm visc}=2\,\tilde{\nu}\,(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\tilde{\sigma}_{ij}+\tilde{\xi}\,(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,\tilde{\theta}\,\delta^{ij}\,,

(142)

where the comoving kinematic and bulk viscosity coefficients
are
\tilde{\nu}=a^{-1}\nu

and
\tilde{\xi}=a^{-1}\xi
.
The comoving viscous energy flux in the subrelativistic
limit is non-zero for an expanding Universe (see details
in Sec.
4.3
),

\tilde{\Pi}^{i0}_{\rm visc}=a^{6}\Pi^{i0}_{\rm visc}=3\,\tilde{\xi}\,{\cal H}\,(\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho})\,u^{i}\,.

(143)

As we show in Sec.
4.2
, the bulk viscosity is, in general,
several orders of magnitude smaller than the Hubble time,
so we will assume
\xi H=\tilde{\xi}{\cal H}\ll 1
and,
hence,
\tilde{\Pi}^{i0}_{\rm visc}\simeq 0
.

On the other hand, the contribution to the energy conservation equation
due to heat fluxes
q_{i}

is described by an additional term in the momentum density,

T^{i0}=T^{i0}_{\rm pf}-\Pi^{i0}_{\rm heat}
.
According to the Fourier’s law
of conductivity, also found following first-order Chapman-Enskog theory,
the subrelativistic limit of the deviatoric momentum tensor is

Ahonen (1999)
;
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)

\Pi^{i0}_{\rm heat}=-q^{i}U^{0}\simeq-a^{-1}q^{i}\,,\qquad\text{where \ }q^{i}=-\kappa\,g^{ij}\partial_{j}T=-a^{-2}\,\kappa\,\partial_{i}T\,,

(144)

being
\kappa
the thermal conductivity.
The comoving deviatoric momentum density is

\tilde{\Pi}^{i0}_{\rm heat}=a^{6}\Pi^{i0}_{\rm heat}=-\tilde{q}^{i}=\tilde{\kappa}\,\partial^{i}\tilde{T}\,,

(145)

where
\tilde{q}^{i}=a^{5}q^{i}=a^{3}q_{i}

is the comoving heat flux,
\tilde{\kappa}=a^{2}\kappa
the comoving thermal conductivity, and
\tilde{T}=aT
the comoving temperature.

4.1.1 Conservation form of Navier-Stokes equations with thermal conductivity

For a fluid description of the primordial plasma in an expanding Universe,
Navier-Stokes and Fourier descriptions for viscosity
and heat fluxes
lead to the
inclusion of Eq. (
140
) in the momentum equation [cf. Eq. (
83
)],

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{0i}_{\rm pf}+\partial_{j}\tilde{T}^{ij}_{\rm pf}=\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}+\partial_{j}\tilde{\Pi}^{ij}_{\rm visc}=\tilde{f}_{H}^{i}+\tilde{f}^{i}_{{\rm ipf}}\,,

(146)

where we have defined the force exerted in the imperfect (ipf) fluid due to out-of-equilibrium
effects
7
7

7

We have neglected in
\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{\mu}
the force arising from the heat
conductivity,
\tilde{f}^{i}_{\rm heat}=\partial_{\tau}\tilde{\Pi}^{i0}_{\rm heat}=\tilde{\kappa}\,\partial_{\tau}\partial^{i}\tilde{T}\simeq 0
.
In general, from the heat equation (i.e., for a fluid at rest),
\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}\propto\partial_{i}\partial^{i}\tilde{T}
, such that
\tilde{f}_{\rm heat}^{i}

becomes proportional to the gradients of
\tilde{T}
at cubic order, which is suppressed compared to the quadratic
order we consider for first-order hydrodynamics.

\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{\mu}\equiv\partial_{i}\tilde{\Pi}^{i\mu}

and
we set
\alpha=1
(conformal time) for compactness.
Then, the viscous force
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}
corresponds to the divergence of the deviatoric
viscous tensor and takes the following form,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{{\rm ipf}}}{\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho}}=\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\tilde{\boldsymbol{\Pi}}_{\rm visc}}{\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho}}=

\displaystyle\,\tilde{\nu}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\bigl({\textstyle{1\over 3}}\tilde{\nu}+\tilde{\xi}\bigr)\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\tilde{\theta}+\bigl[\bigl(2\,\tilde{\nu}\,\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}+\tilde{\xi}\,\tilde{\theta}\,{\boldsymbol{I}}\bigr)\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\bigr]\ln\tilde{\rho}\,,

(147)

where we already normalize with the enthalpy
\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho}
as this
term will appear in the momentum equation Eq. (
100b
),
and assume homogeneous

\tilde{\nu}
,
\tilde{\xi}
, and

c_{\rm s}^{2}
in the last equality.

The energy equation also includes the effect of
energy dissipation,
\partial_{i}\tilde{\Pi}^{0i}_{\rm heat}=\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}}{}
,
which irreversibly converts mechanical to thermal energy,

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{00}_{\rm pf}+\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{0i}_{\rm pf}=\tilde{f}_{H}^{0}+\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}\,,\qquad\text{where \ }\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}}{}=\tilde{\kappa}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}^{2}\tilde{T}\,,

(148)

and we have assumed homogeneous
\tilde{\kappa}
in the last equality.
The temperature can be directly related to the energy density using Eq. (
156
)
for a radiation-dominated fluid.

To solve this system of equations in the conservation form, one can follow the procedure
described in Sec.
3.4
to relate
\tilde{T}^{ij}_{\rm pf}
to
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}_{\rm pf}
, and then construct the viscous imperfect
four-force
\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{\rm ipf}
from Eqs. (
147
) and (
148
),
where the velocity field can be obtained from
\tilde{T}^{0\mu}_{\rm pf}
using Eq. (
89
).
Then, the viscous force
\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{\rm ipf}
can be included in the force array
{\cal F}^{a}

in Eq. (
85
), as well as any additional forces that might be
exerted on the fluid (e.g., the Lorentz force due to electromagnetic fields, as we will see
in Sec.
6
).

4.1.2 Non-conservation form of Navier-Stokes equations with thermal conductivity

In the following, we include the imperfect (viscous) force
\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{\mu}
in the
non-conservation form of the energy and momentum equations,
studied for perfect fluids in Sec.
3.5
.
To find the effect of viscous forces,
we first note that any external forces lead to an additional term in the evolution
equation of the Lorentz factor [cf. Eq. (
119
)] as they
produce work against the fluid.
The resulting dynamical equation for
\gamma^{2}
is

[D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}]_{\rm ipf}=[D_{\tau}\ln\gamma^{2}]_{\rm pf}-\frac{2\,u^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}}{\tilde{\rho}}+\frac{2}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,\frac{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}}{\tilde{\rho}}\,.

(149)

The energy dissipation due to the deviations with respect to LTE
is usually neglected in numerical studies of MHD evolution in the primordial Universe
(cf.
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2017a)
), although it has been
incorporated in theoretical studies
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
.
However, since the shear viscosity and thermal conductivity are in general small,
imperfect fluid effects on the evolution of
u^{2}
can be neglected at large scales.
The dissipation due to thermal conductivity corresponds to
\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}=\tilde{\kappa}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}^{2}\tilde{T}
[cf. Eq. (
148
)].
The dissipation due to viscous forces can be computed
taking into account the following property of the viscous deviatoric
stress tensor under the CIT approach,
U_{\nu}\Pi_{\rm visc}^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}\equiv U_{\nu}f_{\rm visc}^{\nu}=-2\,\eta_{\rm visc}\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}S_{\mu\nu}-\zeta_{\rm visc}\,\theta^{2}

Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
, where
\sigma^{\mu\nu}=S^{\mu\nu}-{\textstyle{1\over 3}}\,\theta\,h^{\mu\nu}

is the covariant generalization of the Navier-Stokes viscosity given
in Eq. (
140
) (see Sec.
4.3
).
Omitting
\tilde{f}_{\rm visc}^{0}=\partial_{i}\tilde{\Pi}^{0i}_{\rm visc}
for negligible
bulk viscosity [cf. Eq. (
143
)],
the subrelativistic version of this identity allows us to compute
the work done by the viscous forces

\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm visc}}{\tilde{p}+\tilde{\rho}}\simeq-2\,\tilde{\nu}\,\tilde{\sigma}^{ij}\tilde{S}_{ij}-\tilde{\xi}\,\tilde{\theta}^{2}=-2\,\tilde{\nu}\,\tilde{S}^{ij}\tilde{S}_{ij}+\bigl({\textstyle{2\over 3}}\tilde{\nu}-\tilde{\xi}\bigr)\,\tilde{\theta}^{2}\,,

(150)

which corresponds to the classical energy dissipation that appears when one takes
the second moment of the subrelativistic Boltzmann equation
Kundu and Cohen (2008)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
;
Cercignani and Kremer (2002)
.
It
represents the irreversible conversion of mechanical to thermal
energy through the action of viscous stresses.
As these correspond to out-of-equilibrium effects, both
\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\tilde{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle q$}}}{}

and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}=-2\,\tilde{\eta}_{\rm visc}\,\tilde{\sigma}^{ij}\,\tilde{S}_{ij}-\tilde{\zeta}_{\rm visc}\,\tilde{\theta}^{2}
are irreversible processes leading to
an increase of entropy.

The resulting relativistic energy and momentum equations are found including the
divergence of the imperfect fluid stresses and momentum fluxes
to Eqs. (
107
) and (
113
),

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,=

\displaystyle\,-\frac{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}

\displaystyle\,+\frac{1}{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2}}\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}\bigl[\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}(1+u^{2})-2\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}\bigr]+{\cal F}_{H}^{0}\,,

(151a)

\displaystyle D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{(1-c_{\rm s}^{2}u^{2})\gamma^{2}}\biggl[c_{\rm s}^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+c_{\rm s}^{2}\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}-\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggl(\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}-\frac{2\,c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\,-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}}{\gamma^{2}}+\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}}{\tilde{\rho}\gamma^{2}}+\boldsymbol{\cal F}_{H}\,,

(151b)

where the Hubble friction
{\cal F}_{H}^{0}
appearing in the energy equation
is given for a generic choice of
\beta
in Eq. (
108
), and
for
\beta=4
, it can be simplified to Eq. (
108
).
Note that the system of Eqs. (
151
) can be generalized to any set
of external forces
\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{\rm tot}
by setting
\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{\mu}\to\tilde{f}_{\rm tot}^{\mu}
.
In Sec.
6
, we will include the
contribution from the Lorentz force.

In the subrelativistic limit, we find

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}\partial_{\tau}\ln\tilde{\rho}\,=-

\displaystyle\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-(1-c_{\rm s}^{2})(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\,\frac{1}{\tilde{\rho}}(\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}-2\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf})+\bigl[\beta-3\,(1+c_{\rm s}^{2})\bigr]\,{\cal H}\,,

(152a)

\displaystyle\lim_{u^{2}\ll 1}D_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}=

\displaystyle\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,c_{\rm s}^{2}\biggl[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\frac{1-c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{})\ln\tilde{\rho}\biggr]-\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}}{\tilde{\rho}}\biggl(\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}-\frac{2\,c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}\biggr)

\displaystyle-\frac{c_{\rm s}^{2}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\ln\tilde{\rho}+\frac{1}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\frac{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}}{\tilde{\rho}}+(3c_{\rm s}^{2}-1)\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\,{\cal H}\,,

(152b)

where the term in the momentum equation proportional to
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}
is the term that had been included in previous work studying Navier-Stokes in a radiation-dominated expanding Universe.
Considering Navier-Stokes description for the viscosity
and Fourier’s law for the thermal conductivity,
the imperfect (viscous) force can be described
using Eq. (
147
), and the heat dissipation
\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{0}
using Eq. (
148
).
The work done by the viscous forces
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{f}$}}{}_{\rm ipf}
can be computed
using Eq. (
150
).
Under the assumption that the
shear viscosity is very small in the early Universe
Arnold et al. (2000)
,
previous work neglected the imperfect-fluid dissipation terms in
the energy and momentum equations.

4.2
Transport coefficients in the primordial plasma

The viscous transport coefficients
\nu
and
\xi
,
and the thermal conductivity
\kappa
,
are proportional to the mean free path
l_{\rm mfp}
of a
plasma composed by radiation particles

Weinberg (1971)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Ahonen (1999)
,

\nu=\frac{4}{15}\frac{l_{\rm mfp}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,,\qquad\xi=4\,\bigl({\textstyle{1\over 3}}-c_{\rm s}^{2}\bigr)\frac{l_{\rm mfp}}{1+c_{\rm s}^{2}}\,,\qquad\kappa=\frac{4}{3}\frac{\rho}{T}l_{\rm mfp}\,.

(153)

In the early Universe, the viscosity and thermal conductivity
are expected to be determined by the
neutrinos mean free path for temperatures below the weak W-boson mass,
T\lesssim 80

GeV
Arnold et al. (2000)
, down to the neutrino decoupling at
T_{\nu}\simeq
1 MeV.
The mean free path of neutrinos can be expressed as
Heckler and Hogan (1993)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Durrer and Neronov (2013)

l_{\rm mfp}(80\,{\rm GeV}>T>1\,{\rm MeV})\approx l_{\nu,{\rm mfp}}\approx n_{f}^{-1}G_{F}^{-2}T^{-2}\simeq 11\,{\rm cm}\,\biggl(\frac{14}{g_{f}}\biggr)\,\biggl(\frac{100\,{\rm MeV}}{T}\biggr)^{5}\,,

(154)

where
G_{F}\simeq 1.166\times 10^{-5}\,{\rm GeV}^{-2}
is the Fermi constant
that determines the strength of the weak interactions at
T\lesssim 80
GeV,

\zeta
is the Riemann zeta function, and the number density of fermions is
n_{f}=3\,\zeta(3)/(4\pi^{2})\,g_{f}\,T^{3}
, with
g_{f}=14
accounting for
muons, neutrinos, and electrons.
Therefore, the
shear viscosity is in general tiny
at high temperatures when compared to the Hubble scale

Caprini et al. (2009)
;
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
;
Brandenburg et al. (2017a)
;
Roper Pol et al. (2020b)
,

\nu H(80\,{\rm GeV}>T>1\,{\rm MeV})\simeq 6.5\times 10^{-7}\biggl(\frac{14}{g_{f}}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{g_{\ast}}{17.25}\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\frac{100\,{\rm MeV}}{T}\biggr)^{3}\,,

(155)

where
g_{\ast}=g_{b}+\tfrac{7}{8}g_{f}
is the number of relativistic degrees of freedom,
with
g_{b}=5
accounting for photons and pions
\pi^{\pm,0}
(
g=3
), which are the lightest mesons
and remain
relativistic at
T\simeq 100
MeV
Kolb and Turner (1990)
.
The Hubble rate
H

can be computed using Eq. (
44
) taking into account that the
energy density is dominated by radiation,

H=\sqrt{\frac{\rho}{3M_{\rm pl}^{2}}}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }\rho=\frac{\pi^{2}}{30}g_{\ast}T^{4}\,.

(156)

Indeed, we find that
\nu H\sim 10^{-7}
around the QCD scale at 100 MeV and
it decreases proportional to
T^{-3}
at higher temperatures, with
some moderate dependence on the degrees of freedom
g_{\ast}
and
g_{f}
,
becoming
\nu H\sim 10^{-16}
around the electroweak scale at 100 GeV when
g_{\ast}=96.25
and
g_{f}=78
, counting
for all quarks, leptons, and bosons of the Standard Model, excluding the top quark.
We show the value of
\nu H
for a temperature range of
T_{\nu}\simeq 1{\rm\,MeV}<T<1
TeV in Fig.
3
.
Therefore, the primordial plasma can be closely treated as a perfect fluid.
However, dissipative viscous and resistive
effects need to be included for a realistic turbulent
description of the evolution of the
fluid perturbations, especially in the context of MHD
(see Sec.
5.3
)
and decay of primordial
magnetic fields
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
.

At temperatures higher than 80 GeV, neutrino interactions are no longer suppressed and their
mean free path stops being the longest one in the
primordial Standard Model plasma.
At these temperatures, the shear dynamic viscosity is estimated to be

\eta_{\rm visc}\simeq C_{\rm visc}\,g^{-4}_{\rm hyper}\,T^{3}/\ln g^{-1}_{\rm hyper}

Arnold et al. (2000)
;
Arnold et al. (2003)
,
being
g_{\rm hyper}\simeq 0.36

the hypercharge coupling at the EW scale
and
C_{\rm visc}\simeq 16
a constant
that depends on the plasma particle content
(see Eq. (108) of App. A1 in
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
).
Since this estimate omits the collisions
of neutrinos, it is no longer valid to describe the
shear and bulk viscosity
at
T\lesssim 80
GeV, as discussed above.
In this regime, the shear viscosity becomes
Durrer and Neronov (2013)

\nu(T>80\,{\rm GeV})\simeq\frac{20.6}{T}\frac{100}{g_{\ast}}\,,

(157)

which is shown in Fig.
3
, where
\nu H\sim T
.

Figure 3
:

Ratio of the shear viscosity
\nu
[cf. Eq. (
153
)] to the Hubble time
H^{-1}
[cf. Eq. (
156
)] at temperature scales above neutrino decoupling
T>T_{\nu}\sim 1
MeV.
The temperatures at which the top (t), bottom (b), and charm (c) quarks, and tauons (
\tau^{-}
)
and muons (
\mu^{-}
) become
non-relativistic (
T<m
) are indicated with vertical lines, as well as the temperature
scales of the electroweak (EW) and QCD phase transitions (PTs). During the EWPT, around 100 GeV,
the
electroweak gauge (
W^{\pm}
,
Z^{0}
) and Higgs (
H^{0}
) bosons become massive, and during the QCD transition (
T\simeq
150 MeV),
the up (u), down (d), and strange (s) quarks and gluons (g) confine, forming baryons
and mesons, from which only pions
\pi^{\pm,0}
remain relativistic.
For temperatures below the W-boson mass,
T<80
GeV, the shear viscosity is
determined by the neutrino mean free path, given in Eq. (
154
).
For larger
T
, we use
\eta_{\rm visc}\simeq C_{\rm visc}\,g^{-4}_{\rm hyper}\,T^{3}/\ln g^{-1}_{\rm hyper}

Arnold et al. (2000)

with
g_{\rm hyper}\simeq 0.36

being the hypercharge coupling at the EW scale, resulting in
\nu\simeq 21/T
(see Eq. (
157
) and
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
).

On the other hand, for a perfectly conformal theory,
c_{\rm s}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 3}}

and the bulk viscosity
vanishes,
\zeta_{\rm visc}=0
.
However, contributions from particle number violation yield
a leading-log order contribution at large temperatures,

\zeta_{\rm visc}\sim g^{2}T^{3}/\ln g^{-1}

Arnold et al. (2006)
, with
g
being the
appropriate coupling constant at large
T
.
This is in any case generally suppressed compared to the shear viscosity

\eta_{\rm visc}/\zeta_{\rm visc}=\nu/\xi\sim g^{-6}\gg 1
.
When the bulk viscosity is zero (case classically known in fluid dynamics
as the Stokes assumption),
the thermodynamic and
mechanical pressures are equivalent
Kundu and Cohen (2008)

and the trace of the deviatoric stress tensor vanishes,

\Pi^{i}_{\ i}=3\,\zeta_{\rm visc}\,\theta=0
.
Although this is true at small and high temperatures,
there is no particular reason to assume that the
Stokes assumption is generically satisfied in astrophysical systems
Weinberg (1971)
.
Larger values of
\zeta_{\rm visc}
can also arise during periods of
conformal violation, for example during phase transitions
in the early Universe
Arnold et al. (2006)
.
Therefore, although
\zeta_{\rm visc}
is negligible in most scenarios, we keep it
in our equations for generality.

4.3
Covariant formulation of relativistic
imperfect fluids

In the following, the covariant
generalization of the Navier-Stokes equations with Fourier heat
flux
is given, following the pioneer work of
Eckart (1940)

under the CIT
approach
Misner et al. (1973)
;
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Font (2008)
;
Subramanian (2010)
;
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
.
For imperfect relativistic fluids, the definition of the four-velocity is
not unique.
Two common choices correspond to the
Eckart
(U^{N}_{\mu},
where N stands for number density)
Eckart (1940)
or the Landau
(
U^{E}_{\mu}
, where E stands for energy)
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
frames
(see discussion in
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
).
These frames can be understood as those where, respectively, the
charge number density
N^{\mu}
or the stress-energy tensor
T^{\mu\nu}

are at rest, i.e.,

U_{\mu}^{N}N^{\mu}=-n
in the Eckart frame,
and the four-velocity is an eigenvector of
T^{\mu\nu}
(
U_{\mu}^{E}T^{\mu\nu}=-\rho U^{\nu}
)
in the Landau frame.
This implies that either

U_{\mu}^{N}\delta N^{\mu}=0

or
U_{\mu}^{E}\Pi^{\mu\nu}=0
, where

\delta N^{\mu}

corresponds to the modifications of
N^{\mu}
due to deviations with respect to LTE.
For a perfect fluid, both choices coincide
U_{\mu}^{N}=U_{\mu}^{E}
and there is
no ambiguity on the four-velocity definition, but in general, the two velocities
are related to each other by the heat flux four-vector
q^{\mu}

Israel and Stewart (1976)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)

U^{\mu}_{E}=U^{\mu}_{N}+\frac{q^{\mu}}{p+\rho}+{\cal O}(u^{2})\,.

(158)

In a system without conserved charges (zero chemical potential),
as approximately realized in the primordial quark-gluon plasma,
the Eckart frame is not well defined and the natural choice
is the Landau frame
Romatschke (2010)
.
In this frame, the heat current
q^{\mu}
does not appear in the
deviatoric stress-energy tensor
\Pi^{\mu\nu}
but instead it modifies
the charge number density
N^{\mu}=N^{\mu}_{\rm pf}+nq^{\mu}/(p+\rho)
.
Then, both the charge density and the stress-energy tensor are modified,

N^{\mu}=N^{\mu}_{\rm pf}+\delta N^{\mu}
and

T^{\mu\nu}=T^{\mu\nu}_{\rm pf}-\Pi^{\mu\nu}
,
with
U_{\mu}\Pi^{\mu\nu}=0
.
In the following,
we will consider the Eckart
frame, which naturally incorporates the heat flux.
In this frame, the charge current is that of a perfect fluid
and the deviatoric stress-energy tensor,

\Pi^{\mu\nu}
, includes the heat current

Eckart (1940)
;
Ahonen (1999)
;
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Weinberg (1972)
;
Jedamzik et al. (1998)
;
Font (2008)
;
Romatschke (2010)
;
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
,

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu\nu}=2\,\eta_{\rm visc}\,\sigma^{\mu\nu}+

\displaystyle\,\zeta_{\rm visc}\,\theta\,h^{\mu\nu}-2\,q^{(\mu}U^{\nu)}\,,

\displaystyle\,\text{with \ }\sigma^{\mu\nu}=S^{\mu\nu}-\frac{1}{3}\theta\,h^{\mu\nu}\,,\quad\text{and \ }q^{\mu}=-\kappa\bigl(\nabla^{\mu}T+Ta^{\mu}\bigr)\,,

(159)

where
\theta=U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}
is the relativistic fluid expansion scalar,
the
parenthesis indicates symmetrization (i.e.,
2q^{(\mu}U^{\nu)}=q^{\mu}U^{\nu}+q^{\nu}U^{\mu}
),

\nabla^{\mu}T=h^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}T
,
h^{\mu\nu}=g^{\mu\nu}+U^{\mu}U^{\nu}
is the
velocity projection tensor, and
a^{\mu}=U^{\nu}U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\nu}}
is the four-vector
acceleration.

S^{\mu\nu}
is the relativistic rate-of-strain tensor,

S^{\mu\nu}=\nabla^{(\nu}U^{\mu)}\,,\qquad\text{where \ }\nabla^{\nu}U^{\mu}=h^{\nu\lambda}U^{\mu}{{}_{;\,\lambda}}\,.

(160)

We can rearrange Eq. (
159
) in the following way

\Pi^{\mu\nu}=2\,\eta_{\rm visc}\,S^{\mu\nu}+\lambda_{\rm visc}\,\theta\,h^{\mu\nu}+2\,\kappa\,U^{(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}T+2\,\kappa\,T\,U^{(\mu}a^{\nu)}\,,

(161)

where
\lambda_{\rm visc}=\zeta_{\rm visc}-{\textstyle{2\over 3}}\nu_{\rm visc}
.
The terms
\sigma^{\mu\nu}
and
q^{\mu}U^{\nu}
are traceless by construction,

\sigma^{\mu}_{\ \,\mu}=S^{\mu}_{\ \,\mu}-{\textstyle{1\over 3}}\theta h^{\mu}_{\ \mu}=0\,,\qquad q^{\mu}U_{\mu}=\kappa\,U^{\mu}h_{\mu\nu}\partial^{\nu}T+\kappa\,U^{\mu}U^{\nu}U_{\mu\,;\nu}=0\,,

(162)

while the bulk viscosity yields the following trace of the
deviatoric tensor,

\Pi^{\mu}_{\ \mu}=3\,\zeta_{\rm visc}\,\theta\ .

(163)

Therefore, only when the bulk viscosity is zero, the equations of motion of a radiation-dominated imperfect fluid in an expanding background are conformally flat
Subramanian and Barrow (1998)
.
Subtracting the deviatoric tensor

\tilde{\Pi}^{\mu\nu}
to the stress-energy tensor of
a perfect fluid

\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}=\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}_{\rm pf}-\tilde{\Pi}^{\mu\nu}
, we can express the relativistic energy and
momentum equations from Eq. (
65
),

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{T}^{0\mu}_{\rm pf}+\partial_{i}\tilde{T}^{i\mu}_{\rm pf}=\tilde{f}_{H}^{\mu}+\partial_{\nu}\tilde{\Pi}^{\nu\mu}=\tilde{f}_{H}^{\mu}+\tilde{f}_{\rm ipf}^{\mu}\,.

(164)

The solution to this system of equations becomes more cumbersome
than its subrelativistic counterpart, as it involves time
derivatives of the primitive fluid variables in the viscous forces and heat
fluxes
Rezzolla and Zanotti (2013)
,

\tilde{f}^{\mu}_{\rm ipf}=2\,\partial_{\nu}(\tilde{\eta}_{\rm visc}\,\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu})+\tilde{\lambda}_{\rm visc}\,\tilde{\theta}\,\partial_{\nu}(\tilde{U}^{\mu}\tilde{U}^{\nu})+\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\nu}(\tilde{\lambda}_{\rm visc}\,\tilde{\theta})-\partial_{\nu}(\tilde{q}^{\mu}\tilde{U}^{\nu}+\tilde{q}^{\nu}\tilde{U}^{\mu})\,,

(165)

where the comoving variables are
\tilde{\theta}=a\theta
,

\tilde{S}^{\mu\nu}=a^{3}S^{\mu\nu}
,

\tilde{h}^{\mu\nu}=a^{2}h^{\mu\nu}
,
\tilde{q}^{\mu}=a^{5}q^{\mu}
,

\tilde{\eta}_{\rm visc}=a^{3}\eta_{\rm visc}
, and

\tilde{\lambda}_{\rm visc}=a^{3}\lambda_{\rm visc}
.
The time derivatives over
\tilde{\Pi}^{00}
and

\tilde{\Pi}^{0i}
lead in fact to second-order derivatives of the
fluid variables
\gamma
and
\textstyle u
in the energy and momentum
equations.
This would require to readapt the fluid equations if we want to describe
them in a suitable way for direct numerical time integration, such that only one time derivative
appears per equation.
However, since this
is still not a suitable relativistic theory due
to violating causality, as discussed above (see also
Romatschke (2010)
for a review),
in the following we will always consider the subrelativistic limit
of the covariant Navier-Stokes viscosity and Fourier’s thermal conductivity
for simplicity.

5
Standard Electromagnetism: Maxwell equations

In order to describe the dynamics of charged fluids, we first
need to introduce Maxwell equations in an expanding Universe
that will later be coupled to the equations of motion of the
fluid, following an MHD description in Sec.
6
.
In the following, we set
m=1
, i.e.,
the space-like signature
(-+++)
,
X^{0}=\tau
, and
\alpha=1
, for simplicity.

5.1
Faraday tensor and covariant electromagnetic fields

We will only consider the
U(1)_{\rm EM}
sector of gauge fields as the
classical electromagnetic fields,
such that
we describe electromagnetism at temperature scales below the electroweak symmetry breaking.
At larger temperatures, the electroweak force is described by the group of symmetries

SU(2)_{\rm L}\times U(1)_{\rm Y}
.
A
U(1)
Abelian gauge field
A^{\mu}=(\chi,A^{i})
is described
by the Faraday tensor,

F_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}\,,

(166)

which is, by construction, conformal invariant, i.e.,

F_{\mu\nu}=A_{\nu\,;\mu}-A_{\mu\,;\nu}
.
The covariant electromagnetic fields can then be defined in terms of the Faraday tensor in the following way

Font (2008)
;
Subramanian (2010)

E_{\mu}=F_{\mu\nu}\,U^{\nu}\,,\qquad B^{\mu}={\cal F}^{\mu\nu}\,U_{\nu}\,,

(167)

where
{\cal F}^{\mu\nu}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\cal E}^{{\mu\nu}\alpha\beta}F_{\alpha\beta}

is the Hodge dual of the
Faraday tensor,
U^{\mu}
represents the four-velocity
of the observer that measures the electromagnetic fields,
and
{\cal E}^{{\mu\nu}\alpha\beta}
is the Levi-Civita
tensor,

{\cal E}^{{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\varepsilon^{{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}}=-a^{-4}\varepsilon^{{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}}\,,

(168)

being

\varepsilon^{{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}}=\varepsilon_{{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}}

the usual Levi-Civita symbol, taking the value
+1\,(-1)
for even (odd) permutations of
{\mu\nu}{\alpha\beta}
with respect to

1230
and vanishing when two indices are repeated.

This allows for a tensor definition of the electromagnetic fields,
whose vector description is otherwise not invariant under
Lorentz boosts.
In fact, an electric field can be transformed into an electric
and a magnetic field component, and viceversa,
in a boosted reference frame.
We note that the covariant electric and magnetic fields
are orthogonal to
U^{\mu}
,

E_{\mu}U^{\mu}=B^{\mu}U_{\mu}=0\,.

(169)

Inverting Eq. (
167
), one finds

F_{\mu\nu}={\cal E}_{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}B^{\alpha}U^{\beta}+U_{\mu}E_{\nu}-U_{\nu}E_{\mu}\,,\qquad{\cal F}^{\mu\nu}={\cal E}^{\mu\nu\alpha\beta}U_{\alpha}E_{\beta}+U^{\mu}B^{\nu}-U^{\nu}B^{\mu}\,.

(170)

For the comoving Hubble observer with

U^{\mu}=(1,{\boldsymbol{0}})/a
, we find the usual definitions of the electromagnetic fields,

a\,E_{i}=F_{i0}=\nabla_{i}\chi-\partial_{\tau}A_{i}\,,\qquad a^{3}B^{i}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\varepsilon^{ijk}F_{jk}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}\,,

(171)

and zero temporal components,
E_{0}=B_{0}=0
.
We denote as
\chi
the scalar potential and
\textstyle A
the vector potential.
As a consequence of the
U(1)
symmetry of the gauge field
A_{\mu}
,
any transformation of the type
\chi\to\chi+\partial_{\tau}\psi

and
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}\to\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\psi
maintains the physical degrees of freedom (the
electric and magnetic fields) the same.
Raising indices in Eq. (
171
) with the FLRW metric tensor

g_{\mu\nu}
of the space components of the four-electromagnetic fields,

E^{i}=a^{-2}E_{i}
and
B^{i}=a^{-2}B_{i}
.
From Eq. (
171
), we also note that

F_{ij}=a^{3}\varepsilon_{ijk}B^{k}\,.

(172)

The electromagnetic four-vectors measured in the reference frame
of a fluid with peculiar velocity
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}={\rm\,d}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}(\tau)/{\rm\,d}\tau

with respect to the Hubble observer, such that

U^{\mu}=\gamma(1,u^{i})/a
, have the following components

\tilde{E}^{\mu}\equiv a^{3}E^{\mu}=\gamma\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{})\,,\qquad\tilde{B}^{\mu}\equiv a^{3}B^{\mu}=\gamma\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{},\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{})\,,

(173)

where
\tilde{E}_{i}=\tilde{E}^{i}=aE_{i}=a^{3}E^{i}

and
\tilde{B}_{i}=\tilde{B}^{i}=aB_{i}=a^{3}B^{i}
,
given in Eq. (
171
),
are the comoving electric and magnetic
fields, as we will justify in the following
section.

We note that the spatial components of the covariant electromagnetic
fields
E^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}
are not equivalent to the fields boosted
to the fluid reference frame
Jackson (1998)
,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,\gamma\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{})-(\gamma-1)(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{}\cdot\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{})\,\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{}\,,

(174a)

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle\,\gamma\,(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle E$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle E$}}{})-(\gamma-1)(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle B$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle B$}}{}\cdot\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{})\,\hat{\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}}{}\,,

(174b)

and they only coincide in the limit of subrelativistic bulk motion,
such that
\gamma-1\sim{\cal O}(u^{2})\ll 1
.

5.2
Maxwell equations and comoving electromagnetic fields

Let us now proceed to describe Maxwell equations,
which are found from minimization of the electromagnetism
action,

S=\int{\rm\,d}^{4}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle x$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle x$}}{}\,\sqrt{-g}\,{\cal L}(A^{\mu},\partial_{\mu}A^{\nu})\,,

(175)

where the Lagrangian in electromagnetism is
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Jackson (1998)

{\cal L}=-{\textstyle{1\over 4}}\,F^{\mu\nu}F_{\mu\nu}+J^{\mu}A_{\mu}\,,

(176)

and an effective current
J^{\mu}

is introduced to model the current
density produced, for example, by the charged particles in a plasma.
Notice that the term
J^{\mu}A_{\mu}
does not
lead to a gauge invariant definition of the Lagrangian.
However, the current density
J^{\mu}
is assumed to describe a sector whose interaction with the gauge fields is written in the minimal coupling scheme, such that the term
J^{\mu}A_{\mu}
is included in the gauge invariant kinetic term of this additional sector (with
A_{\mu}
coming from the gauge covariant derivative)
Weinberg (2005)
;
Figueroa et al. ()
.

Minimizing the action of Eq. (
175
) in a curved space-time
yields the Euler-Lagrange equations,

\biggl[\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial\bigl(\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}\bigr)}\biggr]{{}_{;\,\nu}}-\frac{\partial{\cal L}}{\partial A_{\mu}}=0\,.

(177)

From this equation, the covariant formulation of
Maxwell equations is found,

F^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\nu}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-g}}\partial_{\nu}\bigl(\sqrt{-g}F^{\mu\nu}\bigr)=J^{\mu}\,,

(178)

where the covariant derivative is described using Eq. (
31
).

As presented in Sec.
3.3
, when two metric tensors are related by
a Weyl transformation
g^{\mu\nu}=\Omega^{2}(x^{\mu})\,\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}
,
then the equations of motion, given by the conservation of a
symmetric

tensor
T^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0
,
are invariant under the conformal transformation

\tilde{T}^{\mu\nu}=\Omega^{-6}\,T^{\mu\nu}
if the trace of
T^{\mu\nu}
is zero.
On the other hand, the covariant derivative of an
antisymmetric
tensor
can always be conformally transformed
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=\Omega^{-4}F^{\mu\nu}=a^{4}F^{\mu\nu}
, as can be directly seen in Eq. (
178
).
For an expanding background, this allows us to map
the results in the FLRW
g^{\mu\nu}
and the Minkowski

\eta^{\mu\nu}=\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}=a^{2}g^{\mu\nu}

metric tensors using
\Omega=a^{-1}(\tau)
.

Therefore, Maxwell equations in an expanding background
can be described as in Minkowski space-time after a conformal transformation
Parker (1968)
,

\partial_{\nu}\tilde{F}^{{\mu\nu}}=\tilde{J}^{\mu}\,,

(179)

where
\tilde{J}^{\mu}=a^{4}J^{\mu}
is the comoving current density.
Furthermore, we will show in Sec.
6
that the stress-energy tensor contributions from electromagnetism are traceless.
Therefore, as long as the trace of the stress-energy tensor associated to
the fluid vanishes, which occurs for radiation-dominated fluids with
vanishing bulk viscosity, then
the MHD equations are conformally flat
Brandenburg et al. (1996)
.

From the conformal transformation
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu}=a^{4}F^{\mu\nu}

and taking into account
\tilde{U}^{\mu}=aU^{\mu}=\gamma\,(1,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{})
,
a consistent
comoving transformation of the covariant electromagnetic fields
description becomes
\tilde{E}^{\mu}=a^{3}E^{\mu}
and

\tilde{B}^{\mu}=a^{3}B^{\mu}
,
as already introduced in Eq. (
173
).

From these expressions, for steady comoving magnetic fields,
the physical components decay with the expansion of the Universe,

B_{i}\sim a^{-1}
and
B^{i}\sim a^{-3}
, such that
the energy density decays like radiation,

\rho_{B}\sim B_{i}B^{i}\sim a^{-4}
(see Sec.
2.6
).
As expected, the decay of the electromagnetic energy density
is absorbed in the comoving
magnetic fields,
\tilde{B}_{i}\tilde{B}^{i}=\tilde{B}^{2}\sim a^{0}
.
These results are identical for electric fields.
We also note that the comoving electric and magnetic fields
can be expressed from the covariant
terms of the Faraday tensor
F_{\mu\nu}

and hence, the covariant gauge field
components
A_{\mu}
are already comoving with the Universe expansion.
However, the comoving electromagnetic fields do not transform like tensors.

For a proper definition of the physical magnetic fields
in the rest frame,
the orthonormal basis of tetrads
e_{(i)}^{\mu}
is introduced,
with the following properties

g_{\mu\nu}e^{\mu}_{(i)}e^{\nu}_{(j)}=\eta_{ij}\,,\qquad\eta^{ij}e^{\mu}_{(i)}e^{\nu}_{(j)}=g^{\mu\nu}\,,

(180)

such that the magnetic field can be expressed as
B^{\mu}=\bar{B}_{i}\,e^{\mu}_{(i)}

Subramanian (2010)
;
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
.
Then, one finds that
\bar{B}_{0}=\bar{B}^{0}=0
and the spatial components become

\bar{B}_{i}=\bar{B}^{i}=aB^{i}\sim a^{-2}\,.

(181)

The projection of
B^{\mu}
along tetrads allows to properly define
the components of the comoving magnetic field
as
\tilde{B}_{i}=a^{2}\bar{B}_{i}=aB_{i}
.
Then, the comoving magnetic energy density is

\tilde{\rho}_{B}=a^{4}\rho_{B}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\,a^{4}\bar{B}^{2}={\textstyle{1\over 2}}\tilde{B}^{2}\sim a^{0}
.

5.3
Covariant generalized Ohm’s law

The current density induced by the charged particles of a fluid is described by the covariant
formulation of the generalized Ohm’s law
Landau and Lifshitz (1987)
;
Subramanian (2010)
,

J^{\mu}=\rho_{e}\,U^{\mu}+\sigma\,E^{\mu}\,,

(182)

where
\rho_{e}
and
\sigma
are respectively the charge density and the conductivity as measured in the fluid reference frame.
The conductivity is indicated with
\sigma
, not to be confused with
the traceless rate-of-strain tensor

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}
defined in Sec.
4
.
In the radiation-dominated era, at high temperatures above the electron mass

m_{e}\simeq 511
keV, the leading-log form of the electrical conductivity is approximately given by
Arnold et al. (2000)
;
Uchida et al. (2025)

\displaystyle\sigma(100\,{\rm GeV}>T>m_{e})=C_{\rm cond}\,\frac{T}{{\rm e}^{2}\ln{\rm e}^{-1}}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }C_{\rm cond}\simeq\frac{12^{4}\zeta(3)^{2}\pi^{-3}N_{\rm leptons}}{3\pi^{2}+32\,N_{\rm species}}\,,

(183)

where e
\simeq 0.3
is the electromagnetic coupling constant,

N_{\rm leptons}
is the number of leptonic charge carriers and

N_{\rm species}
is the number of Dirac fermions weighted by the
square of their electric charge (see Table 4 in
Uchida et al. (2025)
).
The value of
C_{\rm cond}\simeq 12
at
T\simeq 100
MeV and 7
at
T\simeq 100
GeV.
The ratio of the electrical conductivity to the inverse
Hubble time is then extremely large in the radiation-dominated era,

\frac{\sigma}{H}\simeq 5\times 10^{17}\biggl(\frac{100\,{\rm GeV}}{T}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{100}{g_{\ast}}\biggr)^{1/2}\,.

(184)

We show in
Fig.
4
the magnetic diffusivity,
\eta=1/\sigma
, normalized by
the Hubble time
H^{-1}

for the range
1\,{\rm MeV}\leq T\leq 1\,{\rm TeV}
.
Given the large values of the conductivity (small values of the diffusivity),
it is in general
justified considering the ideal MHD limit to study the interplay of fluid and magnetic perturbations at cosmological scales (see Sec.
5.5
).
Even at later times, i.e., at temperatures below the electron mass,
the conductivity of the Universe is still in general very large
Ghosh et al. (2026)
.
Equation (
183
) is valid at temperatures below the EWPT, above which we need to consider the hypercharge conductivity
Arnold et al. (2000)
,

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rm hyper}=C_{\rm hyper}\,\frac{T}{g_{\rm hyper}^{2}\ln g_{\rm hyper}^{-1}}\,,\qquad\text{with \ }C_{\rm hyper}=6^{4}\zeta(3)^{2}\pi^{-3}\biggl(\frac{\pi^{2}}{8}+\frac{20}{3}+\frac{2}{3}n_{s}\biggr)^{-1}\,,

(185)

where
n_{s}
is the number of Higgs doublets (i.e.,
1
in the Standard Model).

We note that the charge density and the electrical
conductivity are both Lorentz scalars obtained by projecting
J^{\mu}

in the parallel and transverse directions with respect to
U^{\mu}
,

\rho_{e}=-J_{\mu}U^{\mu}\,,\qquad\sigma\,E^{\mu}=J_{\nu}h^{\mu\nu}\,.

(186)

Notice that
U^{\mu}
in the generalized Ohm’s law corresponds to the fluid four-velocity.
Let us first consider a fluid at rest
U^{\mu}=(1,{\boldsymbol{0}})/a
, such that
the components of the comoving four-current are

\tilde{J}^{\mu}=a^{4}J^{\mu}=a^{3}(\rho_{e},\sigma E^{i})=(\tilde{\rho}_{e},\tilde{\sigma}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{})\,,

(187)

where we have defined the comoving charge density and conductivity,

\tilde{\rho}_{e}=a^{3}\rho_{e}
and
\tilde{\sigma}=a\,\sigma
, respectively.

Figure 4
:

Ratio of the magnetic diffusivity
\eta\equiv 1/\sigma
[cf. Eqs. (
183
) and (
185
)] to the Hubble time
H^{-1}
[cf. Eq. (
156
)] at temperature scales above neutrino decoupling
T>T_{\nu}\sim 1
MeV.
The vertical lines are explained in the caption of Fig.
3
.

For a fluid with a peculiar velocity
\textstyle u
with respect to the Hubble
observer,

U^{\mu}=\gamma(1,u_{i})/a
,
the components of the comoving four-current density are

\tilde{J}^{0}=\gamma(\tilde{\rho}_{e}+\tilde{\sigma}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{})\,,\qquad\tilde{J}^{i}=\gamma(\tilde{\rho}_{e}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}+\tilde{\sigma}[\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}])\,.

(188)

The temporal component can alternatively be expressed in terms of the current density,

\tilde{J}^{0}=\tilde{\rho}_{e}/\gamma+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{}\,.

(189)

Note that in the subrelativistic limit
\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}\sim\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{}\sim u^{2}\ll 1

and we recover
\tilde{J}^{0}\to\tilde{\rho}_{e}
.
The current density components
will enter Maxwell equations in Eq. (
179
) and the
fluid equations via the Lorentz force
when we consider charged fluids with
peculiar velocities
\textstyle u
with respect to the Hubble observer
in Sec.
6
.
The electric field can be expressed from the current density
inverting Eq. (
188
),

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}=\frac{\tilde{\eta}}{\gamma}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{}-\tilde{\eta}\,\tilde{\rho}_{e}\,\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle u$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle u$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}\,,

(190)

where
\eta=\sigma^{-1}
is the magnetic diffusivity
(see Fig.
4
) and
\tilde{\eta}=a^{-1}\eta
is the comoving diffusivity.
This second form of the generalized Ohm’s law will become relevant
when studying MHD in the limit of large conductivity (see Sec.
5.5
) as this assumption will allow us to close
the system of MHD equations after expressing the electric field
as a function of the current density.

5.4
Maxwell equations in an expanding Universe

We now present the Maxwell equations, with the electric
and magnetic fields computed in the reference
frame of the Hubble observer
8
8

8

For a generic observer, Maxwell equations in a curved
space-time are presented, e.g., in
Subramanian (2010)

or App. B of
Durrer and Neronov (2013)
.
with
U^{\mu}=(1,{\boldsymbol{0}})/a

in Eq. (
173
).
Let us start with Gauss’ law, found when
\mu=0
in Eq. (
179
),

\partial_{i}\tilde{F}^{0i}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}=\tilde{J}^{0}\,,

(191)

where
\tilde{E}_{i}=F_{i0}
and
\tilde{J}^{0}
corresponds to the comoving charge density
of a fluid with peculiar velocity
\textstyle u

measured by a Hubble observer, given in Eq. (
188
).

Gauss’ law provides a constraint on the electric field and, when expressed in terms
of the gauge field
A_{\mu}=(\chi,A_{i})
, it yields an evolution
equation for
\Gamma=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}
,

\partial_{\tau}\Gamma=\nabla^{2}\chi-\tilde{J}^{0}\,.

(192)

Ampère’s law is found from Eq. (
179
) taking
\mu=i
,

\partial_{\tau}\tilde{F}^{i0}+\partial_{j}\tilde{F}^{ij}=\tilde{J}^{i}\Rightarrow\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{}\,,

(193)

where
\tilde{B}^{i}=\varepsilon^{ijk}F_{jk}
.
Taking the divergence of Ampère’s law,
we find the charge conservation equation,

\partial_{\mu}\tilde{J}^{\mu}=\partial_{\tau}\tilde{J}^{0}+\partial_{i}\tilde{J}^{i}=0\,,

(194)

which is a direct consequence of Maxwell equations and the antisymmetry of the Faraday
tensor:
\partial_{\mu}\tilde{J}^{\mu}=\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\tilde{F}^{{\mu\nu}}=0
.
In terms of the gauge field components
A_{\mu}
, we find a second-order differential
equation in time for
A_{i}
,

\partial_{\tau}^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}-\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\chi+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\Gamma-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{J}$}}{}\,,

(195)

which can be solved for a particular gauge choice
\chi=\chi(A_{i})
.
We note that this equation can be obtained directly from Eq. (
193
)
from the Faraday tensor components,

-\partial_{\tau}F_{i0}+\partial_{j}F_{ij}=\partial_{\tau}^{2}A_{i}-\partial_{\tau}\partial_{i}A_{0}+\partial_{j}\partial_{i}A_{j}-\partial_{j}\partial_{j}A_{i}\,,

(196)

or
taking into account that
\tilde{B}_{i}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}

and using the following vectorial identity in Eq. (
193
),

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times{\boldsymbol{A}})=-\nabla^{2}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{}+\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\Gamma\,.

(197)

At this stage, although not necessary,
it is useful to introduce the remaining Maxwell equations using
Bianchi identities.
For this purpose, we note that any Faraday tensor described in terms of a gauge
field
A_{\mu}
satisfies the Bianchi identities,

F_{{\mu\nu}\,;\sigma}+F_{{\sigma\mu}\,;\nu}+F_{{\nu\sigma}\,;\mu}=0\,.

(198)

Contraction of these identities with
{\textstyle{1\over 2}}{\cal E}^{\mu\alpha\nu\sigma}
yields a conservation law
for the dual Faraday tensor,

{\cal F}^{\mu\nu}{{}_{;\,\mu}}=0\,.

(199)

Noting that
{\cal F}^{\mu\nu}
is equivalent to
F^{\mu\nu}
under the transformation

E^{\mu}\to-B^{\mu}
and
B^{\mu}\to E^{\mu}
,
then the Gauss’ law for magnetic fields and
the Faraday’s law can be obtained under this transformation,
setting the magnetic sources to zero,

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}\,.

(200)

We note that these laws are obtained due to the invariance of the Faraday tensor over
coordinate transformations and they do not add new dynamics.
Indeed, we find that these equations are trivially satisfied
when we introduce the gauge field
A_{\mu}
and its relation
to the electromagnetic fields Eq. (
171
),

\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\cdot(\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle A$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle A$}}{})=0\,,\qquad\partial_{\tau}\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{B}$}}{}=\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\chi-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}=-\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\nabla$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\nabla$}}{}\times\mathchoice{\mbox{\boldmath$\displaystyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\textstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$\scriptscriptstyle\tilde{E}$}}{}\,.

(201)

In summary, one can describe the dynamical evolution of Maxwell equations evolving the
electric and the gauge fields, together with a Gauss constraint,
